# CHRONICLE
## O F   T H E
# WALDENSIANS,

*That is:*

Account of the origins, doctrine, and life; as well as the numerous persecutions of the Evangelical Christians, who are called Waldensians <note>The Waldensians, or Vaudois, are one of the oldest Protestant groups, originating in the 12th century. They faced centuries of intense persecution by the Catholic Church for their beliefs.</note>.

Beginning from the year 1160 until 1655 inclusive; in which is included the latest horrific massacre <note>This refers to the "Piedmontese Easter" of April 1655, a brutal massacre of Waldensian civilians by the troops of the Duke of Savoy, which sparked international outrage.</note> committed against the Church of Piedmont.

Written in High German <note>original: "Hooghduytsh"</note> by the highly learned
->D. S.<-
->Professor of the city of ZURICH.<- <note>D.S. likely refers to Johann Heinrich Ott (1617–1682), a Swiss historian and theologian who was a professor in Zurich and wrote extensively on the Waldensians.</note>

And now faithfully translated into our Dutch language <term>Nederduytsche Tale: Literally "Low German speech," which was the standard term for the Dutch language in the 17th century</term>.

<note>The ornament described in the original transcription—a grotesque mask with foliage and fruit—is a typical Dutch Renaissance decorative element used by printers to fill empty space and denote the quality of the press.</note>

->IN AMSTERDAM,<-

By Jaques Boursse, Bookseller, on the Lelygracht, at the Sign of the "Antwerp Exchange" <note>original: "in de Beurs van Antwerpen," identifying the name or sign of the bookseller's shop</note>. 1657.

# THE CHRONICLE
## *OF THE* WALDENSIANS,

->That is:<-

->*Concerning the origin, teachings, and life, as well as the manifold oppressions and persecutions of the* WALDENSIANS.<-

---

### THE 1st CHAPTER.

->From where the Waldensians obtained their name, the circumstances by which they separated themselves from the Roman Church, where they first lived, and how they were later dispersed.<-

<image-desc>Large decorative drop cap 'O' featuring elaborate floral scrolls and foliage within a square frame.</image-desc>

Around the year 1160 after the birth of Christ, there lived in Lyon, France, a wealthy, learned, and godly <term>merchant</term> <note>Peter Waldo (c. 1140 – c. 1205) was a leader of a lay spiritual movement that eventually became known as the Waldensians.</note> named Peter Waldo; he gave great alms to the poor, and while he shared that physical bread with them, he simultaneously exhorted them that they, above all else, [should seek] Jesus Christ, the true Bread of Heaven, and the

<summary>This is the title page and opening paragraph of a history of the Waldensians, introducing Peter Waldo of Lyon as a pious merchant who provided for both the physical and spiritual needs of the poor in the 12th century.</summary>
<keywords>Waldensians, Peter Waldo, Lyon, France, Roman Church, Bread of Heaven, religious history, 12th century</keywords>

--- Page 18 ---
<meta>This page continues the narrative of Peter Waldo's conversion and the beginnings of his spiritual movement in Lyon, following the introduction on page 1 which described him as a wealthy merchant providing for the poor.</meta>

### Chronicle of the

true Food of Souls.

Some recount that *Waldo*, by means of the following tragic event, began to lead a blameless and nearly <term>Apostolic</term> <note>Living in imitation of the simplicity and missionary zeal of Jesus’s original twelve apostles.</note> life.

Once, in the evening after a meal, when he was with several of his good friends and making merry with them, it happened that one of the company died a sudden and unexpected death. They were all greatly terrified by this; but especially *Waldo*, who took this to heart as a visible judgment of God. From that hour on, he dedicated himself to a life of sincere repentance, spending his time reading the <term>Holy Scriptures</term>. He also published several useful and edifying books and taught the poor who were accustomed to receiving alms from him.

In addition to this, he was moved to the diligent reading of the Holy Scriptures and to consider the state of religious affairs more deeply, not only because of the great blasphemies that had become common among the <term>Clergy</term> <note>The body of ordained ministers and priests in the Church.</note>, but also due to the terrible <term>Idolatry</term> <note>The author is referring here to the worship of images or religious objects, which the Waldensians rejected.</note> that rose higher and higher. It reached such a point that anyone was condemned to death who could not believe that, at the word of the priest, the Body of our Lord *Jesus Christ* <note>original: "Jesu Christi"</note>—essentially, as long and as broad as He hung upon the Cross—was present in the small

<meta>catchword: round</meta>

<summary>This page describes Peter Waldo’s spiritual awakening following the sudden death of a friend, leading him to study the Bible and challenge the practices and doctrines of the medieval Church.</summary>
<keywords>Peter Waldo, conversion, Apostolic life, Holy Scriptures, clergy, idolatry, transubstantiation, Lyon</keywords>

--- Page 19 ---
<meta>This page continues the account of Peter Waldo’s opposition to the Roman Catholic Church in Lyon, specifically addressing his rejection of transubstantiation and his eventual exile following a papal decree.</meta>

->### WALDENSIANS<-

<meta>sig: A 2</meta>
<meta>catchword: But</meta>

round <term>Host</term> <note>The wafer used in the sacrament of the Eucharist.</note> was: where one also had to worship the aforementioned Host, falling down before it in the streets and holding it to be a God.

Against such false teaching and ungodly life, *Waldo* took a stand, and seriously rebuked many other abuses of the Roman Church.

The Archbishop of Lyon could not tolerate this; he therefore forbade him from teaching, as he was a <term>layman</term> <note>A member of the church who is not a member of the clergy.</note>, and threatened that if he continued, he would be treated as a <term>heretic</term> <note>Someone holding beliefs that contradict established church doctrine.</note>.

But *Waldo* did not allow himself to be deterred by this, but answered: He could not remain silent in such a weighty matter concerning the salvation of mankind; he must obey God—who commanded him to speak—more than men <note>Waldo is echoing the biblical response of the Apostles in Acts 5:29.</note>.

Upon this, the Bishop wanted to have him arrested; but he could not bring it about, since *Waldo* was greatly beloved by everyone and had remained hidden in Lyon for four or five years with the help of his good friends.

Thereafter, when **Pope Alexander III** became concerned that his prestige and high authority over all churches might be endangered, and that *Waldo* might draw a larger following to himself, he commanded the Archbishop there to proceed against *Waldo* and his followers as against heretics and to completely root them out; thus, *Waldo* and his fellow believers were driven out of Lyon.

<summary>This page details Peter Waldo's defiance of the Archbishop of Lyon regarding the nature of the Eucharist and his insistence on preaching as a layman, resulting in his eventual expulsion from the city by order of Pope Alexander III.</summary>
<keywords>Peter Waldo, Lyon, Archbishop of Lyon, Pope Alexander III, transubstantiation, heresy, Eucharist, religious persecution, lay preaching</keywords>

--- Page 20 ---
<meta>This page continues the account of Peter Waldo and his followers being driven out of Lyon. It details their subsequent migrations across Europe and the spread of their religious movement into various regions, including the Alpine valleys and Eastern Europe.</meta>

# Chronicle of the

But **Waldo** went into the <term>Dauphiné</term> <note>A historical province in southeastern France.</note> and into the mountains of **Provence**, on the borders of **Piedmont**, and even into **Piedmont** itself, where the Churches then quickly increased in number.

It is also believed that these persecuted people of **Lyon**, before they departed from **Lyon**, sent some of their own to the aforementioned places to see where they might find refuge upon their arrival. And when they found not only sufficient space but also that the inhabitants of that region were of the same mind in matters of Faith (which was demonstrated afterward, when a great number of these inhabitants joined those from **Lyon** regarding their religion; about which a Catholic <note>original: "Paeps", a contemporary Dutch term for "Popish" or Roman Catholic.</note> writer, in a certain book published in the year 1636, writes that the religion of the **Waldensians** and <term>Albigensians</term> <note>A medieval religious group in southern France often associated with the Waldensians due to their shared opposition to the Roman Church.</note> had always been in the **Angrogna Valley**), they then moved there.

He also traveled into **Picardy**, where he taught with great success and gained many companions in the Faith. But since they could not remain safe there for long, many of them moved from there to **Germany**. These believers spread out into **Bohemia** (to which **Waldo** himself also traveled with some and where he spent the remainder of his life), into **Bulgaria**, **Croatia**, **Dalmatia**, **Hungary**, and many other places.

But all these were, after **Waldo**, called Walden-

<header>WALDENSIANS.</header>

-sians named: which name they then also retain to this very day.

In this regard, it should also be noted that **Reynerus** <note>Reinier Sacconi, a 13th-century inquisitor and former Cathar who wrote about dissenting sects.</note>, another Catholic <note>original: "Paeps", a contemporary Dutch term for "Popish" or Roman Catholic.</note> writer, admits that the teaching of the **Waldensians** is more harmful to the **Roman Church** than all others, because it has lasted for a long time and, according to the sayings of some, took its beginning in the time of **Sylvester** <note>Pope Sylvester I (314–335 AD), during whose reign the Waldensians believed the Church became corrupted by wealth and power.</note>, or even of the **Apostles**.

And thus, the enemies of the **Doctrine of Christ** must sometimes give testimony to the truth. For truly, the pure teaching of the **Holy Gospel** <note>original: "Heyligen Evangelii".</note> did not first take its beginning in the time of the **Waldensians**, but has always been preserved here and there, especially in **Italy**.

<page-num>6</page-num>
<header>Chronicle of the</header>

were stripped naked, they were called, out of mockery, the **Poor of Lyon**.

In England, they called them **Lollards** <note>Followers of John Wycliffe, often associated with the Waldensians by their enemies.</note>; in Languedoc, **Speronists**; in the land of Albi, **Arnoldists** <note>Followers of Arnold of Brescia.</note>; in Dauphiné, **Josephists**; in Bohemia, **Hussites**, on account of their teacher <note>Jan Hus.</note>; in Flanders, they were called **Turlupins**; that is, "the banished," who must live in the forests among the wolves.

They were also named after the lands and cities where they lived: such as **Albigensians**, because of the city and region of Albi; **Tolosans**, from Toulouse; **Picardisers**, from Picardy; **Lyonists**, from Lyon; **Bohemians**, from the Kingdom of Bohemia.

The names of ancient heretics were also attached to them to make them all the more hated. They were called **Cathari** <note>original: "Catari", from the Greek for "the pure".</note>, because they strove for a pure and blameless life. **Arians**, because they would not admit that the Host in the Mass is God. **Manicheans**, because they taught that the Popes had no power over Kings and Emperors.

**Matthew Paris** <note>A 13th-century English Benedictine monk and chronicler.</note> calls them **Ribalds** <note>original: "Ribaux", meaning scoundrels or lewd persons.</note>, that is, fornicators. Another calls them molesters of boys; **Claudius Rubis** <note>Claude de Rubys (1533–1613), a French jurist known for his polemics against Protestants.</note> says that whenever one wanted to speak of a pimp <note>original: "Cobenaer", an old term for a procurer or scoundrel.</note>, they insulted him by calling him a **Waldensian**.

It was rumored of them that they lived in great debauchery, that divorce was common among them, they rejected **infant baptism**, they worshipped their **Barbas** <note>An Occitan term meaning "uncle," used by the Waldensians to refer to their pastors to distinguish them from Catholic "fathers".</note>, or church ministers; they did away with the right to swear lawful oaths; they...

### C H A P T E R  I I I.

#### Concerning the teachings, life, and church ministers of the ancient Waldensians.

<image-desc>A large decorative initial 'W' featuring floral or leafy motifs within a rectangular frame, spanning three lines of text.</image-desc>
What the ancient Waldensians taught and believed is known from the *History* which **Jean Paul Perrin** <note>Jean Paul Perrin was a 17th-century historian from Lyon whose work "Histoire des Vaudois" (1618) is a primary source for Waldensian history.</note> of Lyon published openly. In his third book, he printed the following pieces of the ancient Waldensians: namely, the instruction of children; the explanation of the **Ten Commandments**; of the **Christian Apostolic Faith** <note>The Apostles' Creed.</note>; of the Holy **Our Father** <note>The Lord's Prayer.</note>; the teaching of the **Sacraments**; of the **married state**; of the visiting of the sick; and of the confession of sins.
   We shall here only present their ancient **Confession of Faith**, just as the same is to be found in the aforementioned writer on page 79; and it reads as follows:
   I. We believe, and hold as firm, all that which is contained in the twelve Articles of the **Apostolic Faith**; and we hold everything that does not agree with these Articles to be **heresy**.

II. We believe that there is one God: the Father, the Son, and the Holy Spirit.

III. We hold as holy and Canonical Scriptures <note>Books considered divinely inspired and authoritative for faith.</note> the Books of the Holy Bible, namely: the five Books of Moses, the Book of Joshua, of Judges, Ruth, the two Books of Samuel, the two Books of Kings, the two Books of Chronicles, the first Book of Ezra <note>Known in modern Bibles simply as Ezra.</note>, the Book of Nehemiah, Esther, Job, the Psalms, the Proverbs of Wisdom, Ecclesiastes, and the Song of Solomon, the four major and the twelve minor Prophets.

The remaining books they hold to be Apocrypha <note>Books such as Tobit or Judith which are not included in the Hebrew Bible; the author notes that the Waldensians rejected these just as the Protestants did in the 1600s.</note>, just as the Reformed Churches do today.

<margin><image-desc>A small hand icon (manicule) pointing right toward the start of article IV.</image-desc></margin>
IV. The Books of the New Testament are the four Gospels, the Acts of the Apostles, the fourteen Epistles of Paul <note>In the 17th century, the Epistle to the Hebrews was traditionally attributed to Paul, bringing the count to fourteen.</note>, the Epistle of James, two Epistles of Peter, three Epistles of John, the Epistle of Jude, and the Revelation of John.

These aforementioned Books teach that there is one unique, Almighty, all-wise, and merciful God, who created all things out of goodness. For He made Adam after His image and likeness; but through the Devil’s envy, and through Adam’s disobedience, sin entered the world, and we have altogether become sinners in and through Adam.

V. Christ was promised to the Forefathers, who received the Law, so that through the same, they might be led to the knowledge of their sins, in unrighteousness and inadequacy...

VI. Christ was born at the time appointed by the Father; namely, when sin had completely gained the upper hand, and not for the sake of good works—for all were sinners—but so that He might show us grace and mercy, being He who is faithful.

VII. Christ is our life and truth, our peace and righteousness, our Shepherd and Advocate <note>An intercessor or spokesperson.</note>, our Sacrifice and Priest; who, for the sake of Salvation, died for all those who believe, and was raised for our justification <note>original: "rechtsprekinge," the legal act of God declaring a sinner righteous.</note>.

VIII. Likewise, we firmly hold that there is no other Mediator and Advocate with God the Father except Jesus Christ. As for the Holy Virgin Mary, we believe that she was holy, humble, and full of grace; and we believe the same of all other Saints: that they wait in Heaven for the resurrection of their bodies on the day of judgment.

IX. We believe that, after this life, there are only two places: one for the elect and the other for the damned, which places we call Paradise and Hell; and we reject Purgatory <note>A place of suffering or purification after death in Catholic doctrine; the Waldensians were famous for being among the first to publicly deny its existence.</note>, which the Antichrist <note>The Waldensians, like many later reformers, used this term to refer to the institutional corruption they saw in the medieval Church.</note> invented and devised against the truth.

### W A L D E N S I A N S.

...are just as well situated for the reading of the Holy Scripture as the Preacher.

Regarding their Teachers, they were for a long time called **Barbes**, that is, Fathers, so that in **dangerous** <note>original: "periculose," from the Latin *periculosus*.</note> times they might not be discovered and brought into trouble; they were generally learned and well-practiced in the arts, languages, and Holy Scripture, as well as in the teachings of the early Church, as can be seen from their remaining writings.

They were especially diligent and industrious, both in the instruction of their students in the fear of God and in works of brotherly love; principally, they applied great diligence and performed great labor in copying the Books of the Holy Scripture for the benefit of their students before printing presses emerged:

For just as they themselves were excellently practiced in these (which resulted from the fact that no one was admitted to the Holy Preaching-office who had not learned by heart the entire Gospel of **Matthew**, the Gospel of **John**, all the Epistles of the Apostles, and a large part of the writings of **Solomon**, **David**, and the Prophets), so they also earnestly commanded the reading of the same to their listeners with such benefit that the monk **Reynerius** <note>Reinerius Saccho, a 13th-century inquisitor and former member of a similar group, who wrote detailed accounts of the Waldensians.</note> and others like him were forced to admit that they had seen some among the common people of the Waldensians who were so learned they could recite entire Books of the Holy Scripture from memory, word for word.

# Chronicle of the

Alongside the **Preaching-Ministry**, they also practiced their **Handicraft** <term>Handt-werck: a manual trade or craft, such as carpentry or weaving, practiced to ensure self-sufficiency.</term>: many devoted themselves to the practice of **Medicine** or the **Healing Arts**, in which they were very famous; with this, they were on the one hand helpful to their listeners in times of need, and on the other hand, were able to provide for their necessary livelihood during their frequent travels.

Their **Preaching**, **Child-instruction**, and other **Godly exercises** were similar to the current practices of the **Reformed churches**.

They held a general assembly of the Preachers every year, particularly in the month of **Autumn**; at these meetings, those who wished to enter the **Preaching-Ministry** were examined. There, those were also ordained who would visit the distant congregations in **Calabria**, **Apulia**, **Sicily**, and other places; there they usually had to remain for two years, until others were sent in their place by another **Synod** <note>A formal council or assembly of church leaders.</note>.

But should anyone doubt what has been related above regarding their doctrine and life—both what has been said of the Preachers and of their Listeners—let them listen to the following testimonies, gathered partly from **Papist** <note>original: "Paepsche." A historical term used by Protestants to refer to Roman Catholics.</note>, and partly from **Evangelical** and **Reformed** writers.

The **Papists** speak of them as follows:

**Jacobus van Riberia** <note>Jacques de Ribera (1544–1591), a French counselor and historian known for his work on the history of Toulouse.</note>, in his **History of Toulouse**, who ordered the **Waldensians** to be persecu-

# W A L D E N S I A N S.

A 7

...writes <note>This sentence continues from the previous page, referring to Jacques de Ribera's writings.</note> that in their times, little regard was held for the Priests and Bishops, seeing as they were mostly unworthy and incompetent: so that it was very easy for the Waldensians, because of the excellence of their teaching, to gain the upper hand.

**Samuel of Cassini**, also a Monk, writes: that all which he has to refute regarding the errors of the Waldensians consists in this: that they deny Holy Mother Church.

**Reynerius**, a **Jacobin** <term>Jacobiner: a Dominican friar, so named in some regions after the Church of Saint-Jacques in Paris.</term> Monk, (*book on the form of Heretics*) <note>original: "libr. de forma Hæret." referring to the *Liber de forma haereticorum*, a manual for inquisitors.</note> and a cruel **Inquisitor** against the Waldensians, intends to slander them vehemently, where he says:

> they have diligently read the **Holy Scripture**, and in their teachings of **Chastity**, **Humility**, and other virtues, they have always given an account, reminding [their listeners] that one should guard oneself against all slander; and to this end, they cited the words of **Christ** and the **Apostles**: so that the women who heard them were amazed, and thought they were hearing an **Angel** speak rather than a **Man**.

Further he says:

> they have taught from the **Gospel** and the **Apostles** how a true disciple of Christ must behave or bear himself, and that those alone are the successors of the **Apostles** who are like them in doctrine and in life; and for this reason, neither the **Bishops** and **Popes**, nor the **Clergy**, who possess the riches of the world, follow the holiness of the **Apostles**: nor can they rule the Church of Christ, since— <note>The text cuts off here; the catchword on the original page is "mael," suggesting the word "nademael," meaning "since" or "because."</note>

# Chronicle of the

...since the Lord **Christ** did not command such people for His Church: Thus the **Apostles** also did not prostitute or bring shame upon the churches through evil examples and a godless existence; but they preserved them as a chaste, pure virgin, just as they received them from the Lord **Christ**.

Furthermore he writes: that the <term>Waldensians</term> <note>A medieval movement of "Poor Men" who sought to return to the simple poverty and preaching of the early Church.</note> led a godly life, being completely honorable in their morals and words, and that they gladly spoke of God and His Saints, and of all virtues: and how one should flee evil and apply oneself to all that is good: so that they were held to be the most pious people.

**Claude de Seyssel**, Archbishop of **Turin**, in a Treatise that he wrote against them, gives this testimony regarding the <term>Waldensians</term>: that they are upright in their life and conduct, and are found to be irreproachable, of whom no one had cause to complain, as they have applied themselves with all their might to live according to the commandments of God.

As for their aptitude, says the Monk **Reynerius**, they have instructed their children, even the young Maidens, so well that they know the Gospels and Epistles by heart.

**Ribera** <note>Jacques de Ribera, a 16th-century French jurist and historian.</note> says, in his History of the **Colosani** <note>Refers to the inhabitants of the Val de Cluson, a Waldensian stronghold in the Cottian Alps.</note>, that their youth were so well instructed in the Holy Scripture that peasants were found who knew the book of **Job** word for word, and some who knew the entire **New Testament** completely by heart.

# WALDENSIANS.

The Bishop of **Cavaillon** <note>original: "Cabaillon"; he was the local bishop during a period of intense religious tension in the Luberon region of France.</note> / who lived at **Merindol** <note>A Waldensian village in Provence that would later be the site of a brutal massacre in 1545.</note> during the time of the persecution / had a Monk / who was considered the most learned Doctor of the Holy Scripture / dispute with the <term>Waldensians</term> <note>A medieval Christian movement that emphasized poverty and the authority of the Bible over church tradition.</note> / in order to convince them [of their errors] / who [the monk] said: that in all his days he had not learned as much in the Holy Scripture / as in this conversation with the Waldensians / who had proven every point of religion to him with the Holy Scripture.

The aforementioned Bishop also had several Doctors / who had recently come from the famous School of **Paris** / hold a conversation with them / to whom so much was answered / that one of them openly confessed / that he had obtained a better instruction in godliness from the Waldensians' teaching of the Children / than he had ever heard from all the disputes in **Paris**.

When the Waldensians / by their enemies / were falsely accused and denounced before King **Louis XII** on account of many great slanders / the King sent one of his Court Councilors / and a Doctor of the **Sorbonne** <note>The prestigious theological faculty of the University of Paris.</note>, his Confessor / to them / to inspect all their Churches: But they saw and could find nothing else / except that they had no Images or Idols / no Mass-vestments / and no Ceremonies of the **Roman Church**: and found / that everything was fabricated / that had been reported about them. They saw and became aware / that they diligently observed Sunday / and the Children /

# Chronicle of the

...they had themselves baptized after the manner of the early church; that they taught the Articles of the Christian Faith and the commandments of God.

When the King learned of this through those he had sent, he swore and said: that the <term>Waldensians</term> <note>A medieval Christian movement that emphasized apostolic poverty and the authority of the Bible, often facing persecution for rejecting Roman Catholic doctrines.</note> were more pious than he and his people.

And when the King heard at the same time that in **Dauphiné** <note>original: "Delphinat"; a historical province in southeastern France.</note>, and especially in the **Valley of Freissinière** <note>original: "Dal Fraissiniere"; a remote alpine valley that served as a refuge for religious dissidents.</note>, under the **Archbishopric of Embrun** <note>original: "Ambrum"</note>, a people lived who, like wild animals, lived without religion and considered the **Roman Churches** to be evil; he likewise sent his Confessor and the <term>Official</term> <note>A judge or administrative officer of an ecclesiastical court.</note> of **Orléans** there to investigate the state of the matter.

When these envoys arrived at the aforementioned places, they examined those people concerning their faith and life.

But the Archbishop of **Embrun**, hoping to enrich himself through the confiscated goods <note>It was common practice for the property of condemned heretics to be seized and divided between the church and the state.</note>, earnestly urged the envoys that they should hastily condemn these people as Heretics, and allow their goods to pass to him as the Archbishop of that land. But they would not do such a thing, for they found the people innocent; and the King’s Confessor said this openly in the inn, before all the people, when he was about to depart: that he wished he were as good a Christian as the worst person in the **Valley of Freissinière**.

King **Francis I**, successor <note>original: "Naesaet"</note> to **Louis XII**, being informed how the Parliament in **Provence** [had treated] the Waldensians at **Merin-** <note>The text cuts off here at the catchword "dol", referring to the village of Merindol.</note>

<meta>This page continues the historical account of investigations into the Waldensian communities. ->### WALDENSIANS.<-

[Merin]dol, **Cabrière**, and other nearby places had been subjected to such great oppression and suffering: he therefore ordered an investigation concerning their Faith and Life, and commanded **Sir William du Bellay**, his Lieutenant in **Piedmont**, to diligently research everything.

The same sent two honorable men to **Provence** <note>original: "Provancen"</note> and had them investigate how the <term>Waldensians</term> <note>A medieval Christian movement that sought to follow apostolic poverty and rejected several Roman Catholic doctrines; they were frequently targeted by the Inquisition.</note> conducted themselves in matters of faith and life, and in what manner the <term>Parliament</term> <note>The Parlement of Aix, which was the regional high court responsible for the increasingly harsh decrees against religious dissidents in Provence.</note> behaved toward them.

They found that the Waldensians were industrious and God-fearing people, who had come from **Piedmont** to **Provence** about two hundred years earlier, and had received uncultivated lands or fields as fiefs. Since that time, they had supported themselves honestly and harmed no one. They were peaceable and neighborly, paying their debts without any legal compulsion, and showing all kindness to strangers. The people in **Provence** also testified that one could recognize the Waldensians among all others by their godly conduct; for no one heard them curse, name the **Devil**, or swear in vain. Furthermore, they did not associate with people who spoke indecently or cursed.

See, in this way, many from the opposing party spoke of the Waldensians themselves.

Let us now also look for a moment at what those who later adopted and confessed a similar faith judged regarding them.

Chronicle of the
<page-num>28</page-num>

brought to high standing. And since he made himself so agreeable to the Roman See with his *Inquisition* and persecution of the Waldensians, this same *Inquisition* Office has remained with his Order to this day.

In the beginning, the Monks had all the power to seize and persecute the faithful according to their wish and will. They used to gather the people by the ringing of alarm bells <note>Original: "klocken-storm," the practice of ringing church bells in a specific rhythmic pattern to summon a mob or the community for an emergency or public execution.</note>, whenever and wherever they wished.

They could even proceed against the Bishops themselves: for every accusation had to be accepted by them as valid; and whoever was of any means was immediately condemned as a Heretic.

And where they had the slightest suspicion, neither father nor mother, nor friend, nor neighbor dared to speak against them.

If anyone had given a Waldensian (to speak of the best case) a glass of water or a handful of straw in the prison, they were immediately executed as an evildoer.

When someone was suspected on account of the teaching of the Waldensians, neither *Notary* nor *Advocate* dared to serve him: and such persecution extended not only during the person’s life, but also continued after death: For they also raged against the bones in the graves, for they were dug up and cast into the fire <note>Posthumous trials and executions of remains were a common practice of the Inquisition to justify the confiscation of an estate from the deceased's heirs.</note>.

The heirs dared not take up any matters of the estate, but had to remain silent and renounce all things.

And so that the Monks might keep the poor people in all places all the better in fear and awe

<meta>catchword: keep</meta>

<summary>This page describes the terrifying power granted to the Dominican Order through the Inquisition, detailing how they operated above the law, seized property, and even prosecuted the dead.</summary>
<keywords>Dominicans, Inquisition, Waldensians, persecution, heresy, posthumous execution, legal power, alarm bells</keywords>

--- Page 45 ---
<meta>This page continues the description of the persecutions and public humiliations faced by the Waldensians at the hands of the Inquisition and monastic orders.</meta>

# WALDENSIANS

In order to maintain their power, they were accustomed to parade their prisoners, leading them in their cloister-walks and processions as if in a triumph; they dressed them in red cassocks <note>A long, loose garment or cloak</note> adorned with yellow crosses, as a sign that they had been condemned for the sake of heresy.

Some were forced to walk about in their shirts, barefoot and bareheaded, carrying a burning torch and wearing a noose around their neck. They had to stand before the church doors but were not permitted to look inside, especially when the Host <note>The consecrated bread used in the Eucharist</note> was being elevated, as a sign that they were not worthy to behold it or to partake in it.

Some were sent across the sea to the Holy Land, others elsewhere; meanwhile, the monks took possession of their property. Even when they eventually returned home, they did not dare to ask how it had fared with their goods, wives, or children.

And how great and terrible the persecution was can be gathered from this: when it was asked whether those who had held the Holy Supper <note>The Protestant term for the Eucharist or Communion</note> with the Waldensians, or had otherwise had any fellowship with them, were to be excused, the monks' answer was that they were not to be excused, since they could not plead ignorance.

For the Waldensians had already been publicly executed and killed for a long time and in many places with great effort and labor, in such a way that from now on no one needed to doubt whether they had been treated rightly or wrongly.

# Chronicle of the

The crafty schemes and cunning of the monks can also be recognized from this: namely, that they refused to dispute matters of faith with any Waldensian <note>A member of a medieval pre-Protestant movement that emphasized poverty and the authority of the Bible</note> in front of the common people. This was out of fear that they might be convicted of their own errors by the Waldensians using the Holy Scripture.

And even if someone had wished to renounce their faith, they still would not have granted him his life, unless he betrayed all others present and his own fellow believers. They did not allow anyone to come to a public defense. They did not promise to save the life of anyone before the people who had been publicly condemned to death.

They said that one must proceed and carry on with them in such a manner that the deed is always held as confessed, and only the circumstances are inquired after, such as: "How many times have you heard the preaching among the heretics <note>original: "Ketters"</note>, and joined in their prayers? In what place did you harbor and shelter the heretics?" etc.

The monks were also accustomed, when they examined the believers, to look into a book every time, as if they already had everything on paper about which they were examining and questioning them.

They also used to threaten the prisoners immediately with death, saying: "Prepare yourself, and renounce your error, for you must die; and you may bear yourself patiently in all that shall befall you." They did not let

<summary>This page describes the deceptive interrogation tactics used by monks against the Waldensians, including the refusal of public debate and the use of psychological intimidation to force confessions.</summary>
<keywords>Monks, Waldensians, Inquisition, interrogation tactics, heresy, Holy Scripture, betrayal, religious persecution</keywords>

--- Page 47 ---
<meta>This page continues the account of the Inquisition's interrogation tactics against the Waldensians, specifically how monks used psychological pressure and false promises of mercy to secure confessions before transitioning to the military crusade in Languedoc.</meta>

# WALDENSIANS.

no one could answer their questions based on the <term>Holy Scripture</term> <note>original: "H. Schrift"</note>: And if anyone moved the bystanders to mercy and compassion, the monks became all the more vehement, and said, with tyrannical words and gestures, that one must not allow oneself to be moved by such fair-seeming flatteries.

And whenever someone issued a denial, the monks said that they would achieve nothing by denying; they held the matters securely in their hands and had sufficient testimony to convict them; therefore, they should just freely confess, and then mercy would be shown to them; by which reasoning they then brought many to a confession.

Those were not only the persecutions of the <term>Waldensians</term> <note>A medieval Christian movement that sought to return to the simplicity of the early church</note> in the beginning—namely, the unchristian procedures of the monks—but also in the times that followed.

But since these evil tricks were not enough to clear the <term>Waldensians</term> out of the way, shortly thereafter, through the urging of Pope <term>Innocent III</term> <note>Reigned 1198–1216; he initiated the Albigensian Crusade</note>, a very powerful army marched against the poor <term>Waldensians</term> or <term>Albigensians</term> <note>A branch of the movement centered in Southern France</note> in <term>Languedoc</term>.

The Pope sent a Legate to this army named <term>Milo</term> <note>original: "Milon"</note>: he had the city of <term>Béziers</term> besieged. The Count went outside the city and prostrated himself before the Legate, praying that he would at least spare the Catholics, and said that he was sorry that he had so many <term>Albigensians</term> as subjects, that his father had always tolerated them, and that he, on account of his youth—

The Count returned again into the City, called the people together, and showed them how he had accomplished nothing with the Legate, since he demanded that the **Albigensians** <note>A dualist Christian sect in Southern France targeted by the Crusade</note> should abjure their Religion and submit themselves to the **Roman Church**.

The **Papists** <note>A term used by the author to refer to Roman Catholics</note> pleaded with the **Albigensians** that they should not be the cause of their own deaths; to which they gave as an answer: they would rather please God the Lord than the Pope, and therefore would not abjure their Religion. When the Legate heard this, he became very angry and ordered the City to be climbed and stormed in the most gruesome manner.

After the conquest, the **Papists** came out of the Cathedral, singing that *We praise thee, O God* <note>original: "Te Deum laudamus" — a traditional Latin hymn of joy and thanksgiving</note>; these same men hewed down the enemies just as well as the **Albigensians**, so that arms and heads of the professors <note>Those professing their faith</note> flew about, and the entire City was filled with fire and blood.

But to excuse such gruesome tyranny, the Monks spread the word that the **Waldensians** had thrown the New Testament over the walls and called out to them: "that is your faith." The **Monk of Sernay** <note>Peter of Vaux-de-Cernay, a chronicler of the Albigensian Crusade</note> puts such false, fabricated accusations in his History, in order to make the believers more hated throughout the entire world.

and that it was no war over matters of Religion <note>original: "Religie"</note>, but an open Murder <note>original: "Moorderye"</note>; and he had hope in God that his innocence and righteous cause would one day be made manifest.

And alongside this, the King had pleaded with the Legate <note>Arnaud Amalric, the Cistercian monk leading the Crusade.</note> that he would please show mercy to the young Count. The Legate promised him as much, but with such conditions that the King departed therefrom very troubled; but the Count resolved to risk everything rather than accept those same conditions. The Legate let the City be stormed with all his might, but he found more loss than profit, and for that reason desired to withdraw.

However, he devised another trick, and had the Count summoned to him by a Nobleman, who promised him with many high and solemn oaths that he would bring him back into the City safe and sound. Upon such a deceitful pretext, the good Count came into the enemy’s Camp, and immediately, contrary to faith and promise, he was taken into custody.

This perjurous <note>original: "meyn-eedische"</note> procedure caused a great lamentation and weeping among his subjects, the Citizens of Carcassonne: and after they had understood from one of their fellow citizens that there was a hidden passage under the earth, they fled through the same with wife and children, and abandoned everything.

The Legate, finding no more resistance, took the City and had the Count thrown into the strongest Tower of the same, in which—

-> # WALDENSES . <-

he died shortly thereafter; but his cities and lordships were given to the Count of *Montfort* <note>Simon de Montfort, the military leader of the Albigensian Crusade.</note>, who was also simultaneously appointed General over the Army; which had now reached four thousand men; the other two hundred and ninety-six thousand had all forgotten the way to return home again.

A short time after the year 1205, Pope *Innocent* <note>Pope Innocent III</note> wished, for the second time, to completely root out the *Waldenses* <note>A medieval Christian movement often persecuted as heretical by the Roman Catholic Church.</note>; at first with sweetness, through the preaching of certain Monks: thereafter with violence, through fire, sword, and open wars.

The Monks could accomplish nothing with preaching, for they were unlearned: on the contrary, the *Waldenses* were well-versed in the Holy Scripture and led a godly life; but the Roman Priests lived in open blasphemies and gave great offense.

The *Waldenses* sent word to the Bishops that their Teachers were willing and ready to openly defend their doctrine from God's word, and desired that a gathering might be held to speak with one another concerning *Religion* <note>original: "Religie"</note>.

The Bishops and Monks were satisfied: they summoned one another to *Montreal* <note>original: "Mont-Reael"</note>, near *Carcassonne*, in the year after *Christ's* birth 1206; and there, Arbitrators <term>Scheyts-Heeren: neutral judges or mediators appointed to oversee a formal debate</term> were appointed by both parties: two Bishops and two *Albigensian* <note>A term often used interchangeably with Waldenses or Cathars in this context to describe the local dissenters.</note> Preachers.

<meta>This page continues the account of the religious disputes between the Catholic Church and the Waldenses, specifically focusing on the escalation of conflict involving Count Raymond of Toulouse and the launch of the Albigensian Crusade.</meta>

# CHRONICLE OF THE

There was dispute especially concerning the **Mass** <note>The central act of worship in the Catholic Church, which the Waldenses often critiqued</note>, but one of the Bishops with his Monks were so cornered <note>intellectually defeated in debate</note> that they all, being at a loss, departed from there with shame and wished to dispute no longer.

The Monk **Peter of Castelnau** <note>original: "Petrus van Chasteauneuf," a papal legate whose assassination triggered the crusade</note> caused a Nobleman, near **Nîmes**, so much grief and trouble that he <insert>the nobleman</insert> slew him.

The Pope wished to blame this homicide on **Count Raymond of Toulouse**; for this reason, in the Year 1208 at the **Lateran**, he caused a **Bull** <term>Bulla: a formal decree or public letter issued by a Pope</term> to be sent out, in which he placed the Count under the **Ban** <note>Excommunication and loss of legal protections</note>; and to all who wished to march against him, he promised indulgence <note>remission of temporal punishment</note> for all their sins: Admonishing also that these heretics, the **Waldenses**, must be persecuted and rooted out much more earnestly than the **Saracens** <note>A medieval term for Muslims; the Pope was prioritizing this internal crusade over the Crusades in the Holy Land</note>, and that their lands should be taken from them and given to the **Catholics** to possess.

Count Raymond offered his excuses, saying that he had not slain the Monk; that he who had done such a thing had fled to **Beaucaire** to his friends, and that violence and injustice were being done to him; especially since his enemies had charged him with such things, etc. But all these excuses were in vain: For the Pope’s army began to fall upon his land in the Year 1209.

In this Army of the Pope were found many Counts and **Prelates** <note>High-ranking clergy such as bishops or abbots</note> with the people under their command, to all of whom the Pope promised **Paradise**. If Count Raymond now wished to keep his land, he had to consent to everything that the **Papal Legate** (who had brought this people with him) proposed to him.

# W A L D E N S E S.

and among others this: that he was forced to swear that he would help to root out the Albigenses <note>A group of Christian dualists or dissenters in Southern France, often associated with the Waldenses by contemporary chroniclers</note>.

But because of this forced burden, Count Raymond was in great anxiety and distress; he was not powerful enough to withstand the Pope, yet he did not wish to be against the Believers; he desired, therefore, through the help of King Philip <note>Philip II of France</note>, to be released from the Ban: he thus traveled to Rome and received an Indulgence <note>original: "Af-laet," a remission of punishment for sin</note>. But when he returned to his land, the Legate and the Lord of Montfort <note>Simon de Montfort, the leader of the Crusade</note> disputed the Indulgence he had brought with him.

Shortly thereafter, the Lord of Montfort caused the eyes to be put out and the ears and noses to be cut off of more than a hundred Albigenses at Montreal and Beron.

When Montfort had conquered the fortress of Minerve <note>original: "Menerben"</note>, situated on the Spanish border, a certain Abbot there delivered a sermon to the citizens, intending to bring them back to the Roman Church; but their unanimous answer was:
> We will not leave our faith, but we reject your faith of Rome: you labor in vain; for neither death nor life shall turn us away from our faith.

Because of this, the Legate and the Lord of Montfort were troubled; they had a great fire lit and burned 140 persons, women as well as men, and these all went joyfully into the fire.

After this, Montfort besieged the city of Lavaur <note>original: "La Vaur"</note> for six months, which he also caused to be scaled and stormed: and having been taken, everyone was hewn down, except for 80 noblemen, whom the Lord of Montfort caused to be hanged.

--- Page 56 ---
<meta>This page continues the account of the Albigensian Crusade and the persecution of the Waldenses, following the brutal siege of Lavaur and the execution of believers by Simon de Montfort.</meta>

# Chronicle of the

In the year of Christ 1210, twenty-four Waldenses <note>Followers of a proto-Protestant movement founded by Peter Waldo</note> were captured within Paris, of whom some were walled up between two walls <note>A form of execution or life imprisonment where the victim is immured</note> and the others were burned.

In the year 1211, the aforementioned Legate <note>The Pope's official representative</note> passed away, and that very suddenly and unexpectedly; a certain Tedese <note>Theodosius, a papal notary</note> came in his place.

This Legate enticed Count Raymond to him with smooth words, saying: He wished to settle his affairs; but when he came to him, the Legate placed him under the Ban <note>Excommunication from the Church</note> anew, not because of the murder—for he had been falsely accused of that—but because he had not rooted out the Albigenses according to his promise.

The Count retreated to Toulouse <note>original: "Colosa," likely a variation of Tolosa</note>. The Bishop, because the Count was under the Ban, did not wish to tolerate him there in the city; over this, the Count became so enraged that he commanded the Bishop, together with the entire Clergy <note>original: "Cleresy"</note>, to vacate the city upon pain of death, which they then did.

The Bishop sought to avenge himself, gathered people together, and marched with Montfort <note>Simon de Montfort</note> before the city; but they were received several times by the sallying forces in such a manner that they had to retreat and were forced to seek better air <note>A contemporary idiom for fleeing a dangerous or pestilential situation</note>.

The Legate marched forward with a portion of the people, fell upon a strong tower, and had one hundred Albigenses burned alive.

At that time, the King of England <note>King John "Lackland," who was Count Raymond's brother-in-law</note> came to the aid of the Albigenses.

<meta>This page continues the narrative of the Albigensian Crusade, specifically the diplomatic failures between the King of Aragon and the Church Council, leading to an escalation of military conflict involving Simon de Montfort.</meta>

# W A L D E N S E S.

In the year 1212, in January, the King of Aragon wrote to the Council which was being held at Lavaur <note>original: "Vaur," a stronghold of the Albigenses</note>, and pleaded for the Count of Toulouse <note>original: "Tolosa"</note>, his son and council, as well as for other Lords and Counts; but all was in vain and to no purpose. For the Council answered him by a letter on the 16th of January, 1212, boasting of many and great mercies which had been shown to the aforementioned Lords—and especially to the one of Toulouse <note>original: "Cholosa"</note>—by the Pope and his Legate; and that notwithstanding this, they had persisted in their abominable heresies, and for that reason, to their great shame, they were now excommunicated and placed under the Ban.

When the King of Aragon now wished to submit to the Pope’s tyranny, and treated of this at Toulouse with Count Raymond and the one of Comminges, the Count of Foix <note>original: "Foir"</note> also traveled there, and dissuaded them with very earnest and brave words from that which they had intended. By this, the King was moved to compassion, took up arms, and sent to the one of Montfort <note>Simon de Montfort</note>, that tyrannical persecutor of the <term>Waldenses</term> <note>A medieval spiritual movement declared heretical by the Catholic Church</note>, a <term>Letter of Defiance</term> <note>original: "Assegh-Brief," a formal declaration of hostilities or a challenge</note>.

Upon this, the one of Montfort sent to various places in Europe, and had the Crusade preached by the Bishops and Monks; for they no longer had to deal only with a Count of Toulouse, Foix, Comminges, etc., but with a mighty King, who had become the head of the <term>Albigenses</term> <note>A dualist religious sect in Southern France targeted by the Church</note>: and if one did not come to his aid, then it would...

Against this, the King of Aragon wrote to the King in France, describing how the one of Montfort <note>Simon de Montfort</note> practiced such great tyranny and, under the guise of religion, sought nothing other than the crown; begging him that he would not involve himself in this war. And this he obtained: for the King in France had not, regardless of this, liked to see his people led to the slaughterhouse <note>original: "vleesch-banck," literally a butcher's block</note> under the guise of the <term>Indulgence</term> <note>A remission of temporal punishment for sin, offered here as a reward for crusaders</note>, nor so many great Lords, his friends, tormented by de Montfort.

In the Year of Christ 1213, on the 13th of September, the King of Aragon brought together, alongside his aforementioned allies, thirty thousand on foot and two thousand horse, and took the City of Muret. But because he was always at the front, four hundred shooters who had hidden themselves in an ambush overran and killed him; whereupon a great disorder and flight was caused. There were about fifteen thousand of the <term>Albigenses</term> <note>A dualist religious movement in Southern France targeted by the Crusade</note> slain, through which they nevertheless did not let their courage fall, but strengthened themselves in the Lord.

At this very time, eighteen of the <term>Waldensians</term> <note>Followers of Peter Waldo who emphasized poverty and lay preaching</note> were burned on a single day within Strasbourg.

Montfort was summoned to Montpellier to receive such dignity: When he had arrived there, and had already entered into the Church of Our Lady, a riot arose on his account, and the citizenry wished to strangle him; but he secretly made his way from there, and therefore those same attempts came to nothing.

In the Years 1216 and 1217, Montfort again took to the field to root out the good Albigensians: he prepared himself to besiege the City of Toulouse; and as he was fearful regarding this matter, the Legate said: Be undaunted, for in a short time you shall conquer and take the City, and strangle the inhabitants within the same; and if any of the Pilgrims <note>original: "Bilgern," a variant of "Pelgrims." This refers to the Crusaders who took a vow to fight</note> (for so were all those called who allowed themselves to be used against the Waldensians and Albigensians) should perish, they shall immediately enter into Paradise as a Martyr. Then one of the Commanders spoke and said: Lord Cardinal, you speak very presumptuously; if the Lord Count believes you, he does poorly: for you and other Prelates and Clergy are a cause of this great evil, and of the present destruction of the Land; and you shall continue to be so henceforth if one gives you any credit.

Those of Toulouse made an earnest sally and slew many of them, and the Count of Foix brought fresh people from Navarre and Catalonia; those of Montfort betook themselves to flight.

<meta>This page continues the account of the siege of Toulouse and the death of Simon de Montfort, the leader of the Albigensian Crusade, following the previous page's description of the fierce resistance by the local population.</meta>

# Chronicle of the

The battle near Toulouse soon begins again. A soldier says to those of Montfort that he should be on his guard, for the enemy is sallying out from the city. Then Montfort spoke—who at that same time was in the Mass—saying he wished first to see his Savior <note>A reference to the elevation of the Host during the Catholic liturgy</note>; and if the priest had not shortened the Mass, he would have been slain at the altar.

Montfort sits upon his horse; the horse itself was shot in the head and runs off with such turbulence that Montfort could not hold it back. A soldier from the city became aware of this and shoots him through a leg; immediately thereafter comes a large stone from an engine <note>original: "geschut," referring here to a trebuchet or stone-throwing catapult</note> which a woman had fired, and it took his head entirely away, on the day after Saint John the Baptist, 1218.

Count Raymond <note>Raymond VI of Toulouse, the primary opponent of the Crusade</note> defeats the enemy and thanks God for the victory.

In the year 1219, the Legate <note>The papal representative overseeing the Crusade</note> writes to the King in France, exhorting him that he should march toward Toulouse with all his might and avenge the death of the Count of Montfort. He promised him that he would bring it to pass that the Pope would have the Cross preached throughout the whole world <note>A formal call for a new Crusade, where participants wore a cross as a symbol of their vow</note> to obtain help.

But shortly thereafter the Pope dies, and the Legate travels to Rome, saying: he could no longer carry out such work on account of his old age.

In the year 1220, on the 6th of August, the monk Dominic <note>Saint Dominic, founder of the Dominican Order</note> also died, who through his Inquisition and preaching against the Albigensians had become very rich.

# WALDENSES.

Here also died precious heroes: Count Raymond of Toulouse and Count Raymond of Foix, with his godly wife, Lady Philippe of Montcade.

Matthew Paris <note>A 13th-century English chronicler and monk</note> says that in the year 1220, the Waldenses <note>A medieval Christian movement often persecuted as heretical</note> in Germany were attacked and defeated at a certain place where they had a marsh on one side and the lake or sea on the other.

In the year 1228, Imbert of Beaujeu—whom King Louis in France had appointed Governor in Languedoc in the year 1226—came from France with a great force against the Albigenses <note>Another name for the dissenters in Southern France, centered around Albi</note>, who had again strongly reinforced themselves and captured several good positions.

Shortly thereafter, the young Count of Toulouse <note>Raymond VII</note>, who was staying there in his city, was pursued with deceitful words by the Papal Legate; and as if he might obtain a good treaty from the young King and his mother the Regent <note>Blanche of Castile</note>, he was lured to Meaux and, being there, was taken into custody.

The *History of Languedoc* writes in this manner: It was a matter worthy of all pity to see such a brave man, who for so long a time had resisted such a mighty people, being led in his shirt and barefoot toward the altar in the presence of two Cardinals and the Royal Envoys from England and France.

Yet it did not stop here; but in addition, great sums of money were [imposed] upon him—

# Chronicle of the

Thus, a sharp persecution began again against the Albigenses: the goods of both the deceased and the living were confiscated—of all those who were adherents to the teachings of the Albigenses.

No one dared to take up their cause; they were everywhere sold out and betrayed.

In this same year of 1228, the persecutions also began in Provence, after the Archbishops of Narbonne, Arles, and Aix had met together at Avignon and consulted on how they might, with the help of the Inquisition <note>The ecclesiastical court established to suppress heresy</note>, bring down the Waldenses, who remained near their bishoprics with daily increases in number. Thereupon the monks, with their Inquisition, began again and seized such a great multitude here and there that they could not find enough prisons to lock the captives in.

Around this time, Emperor Frederick II also vehemently persecuted the Waldenses in Italy, as can be seen from one of his Constitutions <note>A formal legal decree or imperial law</note>, in which he calls them *Gatzaros* <note>original: "Gatzaros"; likely a corruption of "Cathars"</note>, *Leonists* <note>original: "Leonisten"; a name for the Waldenses derived from "Poor Men of Lyons"</note>,

# WALDENSES.

->Speronists and Arnoldists<- <note>Followers of Sperone and Arnold of Brescia, groups often associated with the Waldenses by their enemies</note>; similar Constitutions <note>Imperial legal decrees</note> were also issued in Sicily.

Pope Gregory IX persecuted the Waldenses to the utmost: his Legate <note>A high-ranking papal representative</note> had their houses torn down and drove them out of all Italy; he also sent two Dominican Monks <note>original: "Predicker Monnicken"; the Order of Preachers, who led the Inquisition</note> to Milan, so that they might investigate and destroy everything.

In the year 1229, a Council was held at Toulouse to implement the Inquisition; there it was decided in seven Articles as follows: We forbid all laypeople the possession of the books of the Old and New Testaments, and forbid explicitly that anyone shall have such in the common tongue <note>The vernacular language of the people, rather than Latin</note>.

Around the year 1230, the Waldenses in Germany were cruelly persecuted by a monk named Eckard; who thereafter, being struck in his conscience, was converted and burned at Heidelberg for the true Religion.

At this very time, Conrad of Marburg was made an Inquisitor by the Pope; he persecuted the Believers quite terribly and tested, with glowing irons <note>The "ordeal by fire," a medieval judicial practice</note>, whether they were good Christians.

In the year 1234, when the Count of Toulouse and the Count of Foix were dead, it appeared as if it were entirely over for the Albigenses.

But Trencavel, the Bastard of Béziers, took up the cause of the Albigenses with force, whereby the Pope's Legate and the Bishop of Toulouse were strongly opposed; and he of Béziers held the field until the year 1242, and did the enemy great damage.

# Chronicle of the

When, in the year 1243, the Count of Toulouse <note>Raymond VII, who had been forced to submit to the French crown and the Church</note> had already paid the imposed sum of money—which we mentioned previously—and had arrived back in his own land, there was great lamentation everywhere concerning the Inquisition and the tyranny of the monks. This reached such a point that, not long thereafter, nine monks were slain by the common people. Thereupon, Pope Innocent <insert>IV</insert> came to Lyon and thundered with his Ban <note>An official decree of excommunication and outlawry</note> against those who had killed the monks. The Count of Toulouse had to pay the price for this when he petitioned the Pope for a dispensation and permission to marry his niece <note>original: "Bas"</note>, the daughter of the Count of Provence. He was also refused permission to bury his father’s remains in a consecrated place, as his father had died while under the Ban.

In the year 1250, the Inquisition against the Believers was undertaken with ever-increasing severity; these measures were confirmed and approved by Pope Alexander IV, by Clement IV in the year 1264, and by John XXII <note>The text says John XXII, though the date 1276 suggests John XXI; the author likely refers to the ongoing papal succession supporting the Inquisition</note> in the year 1276.

In the year 1281, Martin IV also looked favorably upon this Inquisitorial tyranny; and at this time, the Believers were persecuted to the utmost, as can be seen from the following document:

WE, William of Gourdon, Captain and Governor of Carcassonne and Béziers, make known to everyone, for the exaltation of the Catholic Faith and the rooting out of her-

In the year 1304, the *Inquisition* was newly established again against the *Waldenses*: for at that time, one hundred and fourteen were burned alive within *Paris*; all of whom, with great steadfastness, confessed the truth.

In the year 1330, the *Waldenses* were bravely <note>In this context, "bravely" or "dapper" implies "severely" or "stoutly" in the archaic sense</note> persecuted in *England* under King *John* <note>The text mentions King Joan (John) in 1330, though John Lackland died in 1216; the author may be conflating dates or referring to local English authorities during the reign of Edward III</note>.

# Chronicle of the

Around this time there were many **Waldenses** <note>A medieval Christian movement that preceded the Reformation, often targeted by the Church for their rejection of papal authority</note> in the **Kingdom of Poland**, who were also vehemently persecuted by the **Inquisition**.

**In the year 1378**, the **Waldenses** within **Paris** were again persecuted; and a great multitude of them were burned at a certain place called **Greve** <note>The Place de Grève, a historic site in Paris used for public executions</note>.

**In the year 1380**, the first persecution known to have occurred in the **Dauphiné** <note>A province in southeastern France</note> against the **Waldenses** was stirred up by a certain **Barefoot Monk** <note>A member of a mendicant order, likely a Franciscan, who went barefoot as a sign of poverty</note> named **Franciscus Borelli**. He had obtained a **Bull** <note>An official papal edict or decree</note> from **Pope Clement VII** at **Avignon** to track down the **Waldenses**. Because they were situated so near to the **Papal Seat**, the **Pope** would not suffer anyone in the **Land** who held him to be the **Antichrist**; for this reason, he commanded the **Bishops** in **Provence**, the **Dauphiné**, and other places where his dominion extended (for there was discord in the **Church**; some held with **Clement** at **Avignon**, some with **Urban** at **Rome** <note>This refers to the Great Western Schism (1378–1417), when two rival popes claimed authority over the Catholic Church</note>) that they should tolerate no **Waldensian**.

This **Monk** summoned all the inhabitants of the **Valleys of Freissinières**, **L'Argentière**, and **Val Louise** to appear before him at **Embrun**, under penalty of the **Ban** <note>Excommunication or legal proscription</note>; and since none of them appeared, nor anyone on their behalf, they were all placed under **Bonds** <note>Legal condemnation or physical imprisonment</note> as stubborn and disobedient. And wherever one of them could be apprehended, he was executed without mercy.

In **Provence**, one named **Albertus de Capitaneis** <note>Albert Cataneo, a notorious inquisitor</note> allowed himself to be used boldly in the persecution of the **Waldenses**.

Around the year **1391**, in **Saxony**

In the year 1393, the aforementioned persecution in **Dauphiné** <note>A province in southeastern France</note> truly began in earnest: for there, at **Grenoble**, one hundred and fifty men were burned, along with many women, and a great number of youths and young maidens.

The Monks took the second part <note>A portion or half</note> of their goods, and the remainder they gave to the secular Authorities: thus many a person, for the sake of their property, was executed as a **Waldensian**.

The persecution was so great that no one dared to give them lodging, nor food, nor drink, nor to do them any other kindness.

In the year 1400, the **Waldenses** in the Valley of **Pragela** were suddenly attacked around **Kermis** <note>A local church festival or fair</note>, partly by the neighboring **Papists** <note>A term used by the author to refer to Roman Catholics</note> and partly by others who had joined with them; and it appeared as though, because of the heavy snow, everything would be destroyed. # Chronicle of the

WE Louis, by the grace of God, King of France, Dauphin of Vienne, Count of Valence and Die, to our dear and faithful Governor over the Dauphiné <note>original: "Delphinat," a province in southeastern France</note>, greetings and grace. It has been brought before us by the inhabitants of the Valleys of Louise, Freissinières, and Argentière, among others, how (even though they have lived until now as good Catholic Christians, and intend to live as such henceforth, without adopting any superstition or the like, which strives against the obedience and the custom of our Mother the Holy Church) certain Mendicant Monks <term>Mendicant Monks: members of religious orders like the Franciscans or Dominicans who lived by begging and often served as inquisitors</term>, who call themselves Inquisitors and Examiners of the Faith, burden them with the improper extortion of their goods and suchlike, and, seizing them, falsely fabricate that they hold and believe certain errors, striving against the Catholic Faith. And they have, for this reason, drawn them into great and heavy legal proceedings, as well at our Court of Justice in the Dauphiné as in other places; and so that they might come to the confiscation of their goods, the Judges and the said Inquisitors, who are commonly Mendicant Monks, under the appearance of the Office of the Inquisition in matters of Faith, draw the poor people before the court without rightful cause; some they put upon the rack <note>original: "pijn-banck," a torture device used to extract confessions</note>, without previous required testimonies, and condemn them for matters of which they have never been guilty. And as has been learned thereafter, they have released some, after they

# WALDENSES.

...having extorted great sums of money from them, and having tortured and plagued them in manifold ways, not only to the great prejudice and damage of the aforementioned Petitioners <note>original: "Supplicanten," referring to the Waldensian inhabitants seeking royal protection</note>, but also to the damage of ourselves and of the entire Community of our Lands of the Dauphiné <note>original: "Delphinats"</note>.

Therefore, we wish to prevent such things, and not permit our poor Subjects to be tortured and plagued in such an improper manner, etc. We have thus, with held good and deliberate counsel, on account of these deceptions and abuses, tortures and improper extortions, given to the aforementioned Petitioners, and do give out of good conscience and special will, full power; by Royal and Dauphinal Highness we have willed and ordained, and do will and ordain by virtue of this present Letter: That the same Petitioners, and all others of our Land of the Dauphiné, shall be freed and cleared from all Court judgments and legal claims.

We have also, according to our good conscience, full power, and Dauphinal Highness, taken away and dismissed all and every person's legal proceedings with which anyone might be burdened; we take them away, dismiss, and annul the same through this present Letter. It is also our will, that from the beginning, and from all elapsed time until this very day, nothing shall be demanded of them, neither in body nor goods, nor shall anyone be condemned or outlawed. We also hereby expressly command and order you, that you [execute] this our Letter, according to—

# Chronicle of the

...to its entire contents you shall comply, for which we then give you full power and authority, as well as commission and special command. We also command and order all our Judges, Officials, Subjects, and appointees, that they likewise apply themselves to obediently comply with all of this.

->Given at Arras, the 18th of May, 1478.<-

From this the Christian Reader sees how these innocent people were dealt with; and one might have rightly hoped the King's command would have stopped and stilled the raging and raving of the enemies. But no. For the Archbishop of Embrun <note>original: "Ambron"; Jean Baile, Archbishop of Embrun, was a notorious persecutor of the Waldenses</note> became all the more heated against the Believers, and took pride in certain words which were in the Royal Letter, reading thus: *But in case anyone were rebellious in such a manner, and wished to defy in a stiff-necked way:* And he said he did not act against the contents of the Letter, seeing that those against whom he proceeded <note>original: "procideerde"</note> were such stubborn and neck-strong people, who had never wished to appear so that their defense might have been heard.

The Archbishop had also forced a good number of the inhabitants of the aforementioned Valleys <note>The Alpine valleys of the Dauphiné, such as Val Louise and Freissinières</note> to say that no one else was tainted with error <note>meaning heresy</note> except precisely those who had handed the Petition <note>original: "Supplicatie"</note> to the King; and the Archbishop used for this no other witnesses <note>original: "getuygen"</note> than his Priests and Officers, who, as sworn...

# WALDENSES.

...witnesses against the Believers; they allowed these to be used, and said that they had all been **Waldenses** <note>A medieval Christian movement often persecuted as heretical by the Catholic Church</note> who had presented the **Petition** to the King. In addition to this, the **Archbishop** corrupted and bribed with money a certain **Joan Pelgrim**, who accused the **Waldenses** regarding certain alleged matters—namely, the kind of things that people in former times used to falsely charge the first **Christians** with, as if they led a shameful and unchaste life in their assemblies. The **Archbishop** sent him to the King to make his own conceived opinion known: namely, that he did not oppose the **Waldenses** so vehemently for the sake of their property (to take it for himself), but for the sake of the Faith. And one witness was not able to stand against so many who, against their conscience, were willing to speak the slightest word against the **Waldenses**.

The **Archbishop** continues with his tyranny against the Believers; and because they could not afford the costs to go to law with him, they fled out of the country—except for one named **Jacob Poliveri**, who protested against such unjust proceedings and demanded a copy of everything that had been undertaken against him. The **Archbishop** left that man in peace and turned his attention to those who did not dare to resist his power. But how it ended with the two **Burgomasters** <note>The chief magistrates or mayors of the local towns</note> shall be told in the final **Chapter**.

Upon the **Archbishop**, **God** exercised His vengeance: for he died shortly after this committed tyranny.

How gruesome this Bishop’s Inquisitor, the Monk *Veyleti*, has been, one has found recorded in the old Registers and Proceedings at *Ambrun* <note>Embrun, a city in the French Alps</note>: when in the year 1585, many old Papers and Books from the Archbishop’s Chancery were thrown out onto the street. Whenever the Believers answered "No" simply to the Monk’s questions, he was accustomed to add much more and other things thereto, so that he might make them hated through crafty elaborations; as for example: when he asked whether they did not believe that the Body of *Christ* <note>original: "Lichaem Christi"</note>, upon the words spoken by the Priest, was essentially in the *Host* <note>The bread used in the Eucharist</note>, as great and as long as He had hung upon the Cross: And they answered No, then the Monk *Veyleti* wrote that they had confessed they did not believe in *God*. *Likewise*, when they were asked whether one should not call upon the departed *Saints*: And they answered no; then he wrote that they had blasphemed the *Saints*, etc., just as one has seen with astonishment in the *Commissioners’* notes and in the writings of this Monk and his *Substitutes* <note>Deputies or assistants acting on behalf of the Inquisitor</note>.

In the year 1488, *Albertus de Capitaneis*, Archdeacon of *Cremona* <note>original: "Cremina"</note>, was dispatched by Pope *Innocent VIII* against the *Waldenses*; he came with a great force into *Dauphiné* <note>original: "Delphinat," a province in southeastern France</note>, overran some Villages, destroyed everything he could get hold of, and plundered everything clean out; but while many good people had fled from their houses into the mountains,

# WALDENSES.

...and had hidden themselves there in certain caves, he had much wood carried before them and set it on fire, by which the poor Christians were some of them suffocated, and some were driven out from there so that they fell down upon the stone cliffs, of whom many remained dead instantly; others broke arms and legs, and were further slain by the soldiers. They found in the caves four hundred children in their cradles, and some in the arms of their mothers, all suffocated by the smoke. The persecution was so severe that they, at that time, desired to exterminate everything.

The same Albertus marched in that same year with eighteen thousand men into *Piedmont* <note>original: "Piemont"</note>, alongside a great number of Piedmontese and inhabitants of the land, who had run together in heaps from all places to murder the pious people and to plunder their goods, and thus to obtain the Indulgence <note>original: "Af-laet"; a remission of temporal punishment for sin granted by the Pope</note> promised to them by the Pope.

The same marched all at once against the Valleys of *Angrogne*, *Lucerne*, *Peruse*, *Saint Martin* <note>original: "Sanct Martijn"</note>, and *Violet*, in the Marquisate of *Saluzzo* <note>original: "Saluces"</note>, and also brought folk into *Vaucluson*, in *Dauphiné* <note>original: "Delphinat"</note>, and rushed toward *Pragela*, so that they gave the Believers everywhere [work] to do, and so that one Valley could not come to the aid of the other.

But God ordained it so well through His Fatherly providence <note>original: "voorsienighepdt"</note>, that the enemies, to their own ruin and downfall, had thus been required to divide themselves.

# CHRONICLE OF THE

For the Believers <note>The Waldenses, a pre-Reformation Christian movement</note> conducted themselves so manfully—with God’s help—that the enemies were defeated everywhere, but especially in the Valley of *Angrogne*.

For as soon as the Believers noticed the enemies' intentions, they occupied the passes and guarded them well. They took long wooden shields that covered a whole man and held the passes; behind them stood the Archers with bows and crossbows <note>original: "arm-rustingen"; refers to mechanical bows or armor-piercing weaponry</note>. When the enemies drew near, the women and children fell upon their knees and cried out to Heaven, saying in their own tongue: *“O God, help us,”* <note>original: "O Dio adjutaci"</note>.

But the enemies mocked them, especially a certain Captain named *Saget*, who, to ridicule the Believers, cried out in the same way; but he was shot and tumbled down from the mountain into a deep cavern, which even to this day is called *Saget’s Hole*.

At that time, another Captain was also [slain], named the *Black One of Mendari* <note>original: "de Swarte van Mendari"</note>, (who, like a terrifying Goliath, had boasted with terrible cursing and blasphemies that he would carry out a horrific bloodbath). He was shot through the neck with an arrow, by which they—as well as by the stones that were thrown down upon them—became so terrified that they all took to flight, and most of them threw themselves down over the stone cliffs and mountains, and were killed.
