has made itself known; namely, when the enemies in the Valley of *Angrogne* came near a certain place that was very favorable to their advantage, a thick fog or mist arose, along with a great storm and a strong wind, so that the enemies could not see where they were. By that opportunity, the Believers <note>The Waldensian inhabitants</note> took heart and fell upon them, putting them to flight, where they were scattered and cast down from the stone cliffs: and whatever the enemies had plundered from them in the Valley, they recovered to some extent; but the Wine and Flour had been poured out.

Around this time, the enemies also attacked the Believers in a certain Congregation located in the Valley of *Saint Martin*. They were seven hundred strong and came like a turbulent flood against the Village of *Pommiers*. The Believers defended themselves and defeated all the enemies, except for one Ensign <note>A standard-bearer or junior officer</note>.

In many other places as well, they were attacked in like manner, but God gave them great victory and triumph everywhere.

Since they now fought against the Believers with such poor success, the Duke <note>Charles I, Duke of Savoy</note> did not wish to allow these, his most obedient and faithful Subjects, to be persecuted in such a manner henceforth. And so that in the future everything might remain forgotten,

# CHRONICLE OF THE

When they now came before the Duke, he received and accepted them quite friendlily, showed them mercy, and—with the gift of a sum of money for the costs and damages they had suffered—let them return home again in peace.

And whereas some of those who bore them ill-will had made the Duke believe that their children had black mouths, double gums, and hairy teeth, he had some of them brought to him, and found that they were very beautiful children, and that he had been told untruths; he was troubled that he had so easily believed those wicked people: he commanded that they, like all his other subjects in *Piedmont*, should be allowed to enjoy and use all their freedoms in rest and peace.

But setting all this aside, the Monks proceeded with their *Inquisition* <note>The ecclesiastical court for the suppression of heresy</note>, especially near *Pignerol*, and they delivered all whom they could overtake to torture and to death; and such persecution lasted, as we shall see, until the year 1532 after the Birth of *Christ* <note>original: "Christi"</note>.

# WALDENSIANS.

The believer in **Dauphiné** <note>original: "Delphinat," a province in southeastern France</note> who was the persecutor was called to another place; in his stead, he appointed a **Barefoot Monk** <note>A member of a mendicant order, likely a Franciscan, who went barefoot or wore sandals as a sign of poverty</note>, **Franciscus Plaireri**. By him, the **Waldensians** were summoned to **Embrun** <note>original: "Ambzum"</note>; and since they did not appear, they were excommunicated by him and persecuted to the utmost. The **Sentence** was pronounced at **Embrun**, in the **Cathedral**, and posted on a large tablet upon the church door. Beneath this, the **Monk** had placed 30 **Articles** of their Faith, filled with many false accusations written against the **Waldensians**; claiming they held lewdness and similar slanders to be no sin, in order to make them all the more hated.

Just as he then, in the **Year** 1492, also drafted false **Confessions** <note>original: "Beleydenissen"</note> attributed to two pious **Preachers**, and scattered these here and there, as if they had admitted that they had become so powerful in their **Religion** because they permitted lewdness and similar sins, etc. And such false **Confessions** he had signed and validated to his advantage by a **Royal Council** and a **Judge** of **Briançon**; just as these very documents, along with other trial records, were found in a sack at **Embrun**.

In the **Year** 1494, two **Canons** <note>original: "Dom-Heeren," high-ranking cathedral clergy</note> of **Embrun** and the **Official** <note>A presiding judge of an ecclesiastical court</note> of **Valence** were sent by the **Pope** against the **Waldensians** in **Dauphiné**. By these men, a certain **Woman** in the territory of **Valence** was asked: Whether she had ever

One of them, after the evening meal, had asked her if she did not know a few people who are on earth, and without whom the world might perish. She had answered that she had heard no one speak of this, except once the Parson of Beauregard <note>original: "Pharheer tot Beauregardt"</note>, who had said there were some few people without whom the world would perish. And they had also said, among other things, that what someone would not like for themselves, they should not do to another; one should call upon a single God, as He alone can help us, and not the deceased saints <note>original: "afgestoben heyligen"</note>. # Chronicle of the

were forced to be: In this their misery, they did not cease for five years to earnestly insist that they might be reinstated in their land and property; which was not granted to them otherwise than on the condition that they would consent to attend the Mass <term>Mass: the central liturgical ritual of the Catholic Church, which the Waldensians rejected as unbiblical</term>.

But this they would not accept, and God finally, through another means, brought them back to their own; which happened in this manner.

There was among them a brave and stout-hearted man; the same went unexpectedly with other exiles and inspected the houses and the property that had been abandoned by them (and were inhabited by the Papists <note>original: "Papisten" — a contemporary term for Roman Catholics</note>); and with a sword, which he wielded in both his hands, he hewed down everything that he found <note>original: "gebonden heeft" — literally 'found bound' or 'encountered'</note> in their abandoned houses, both humans and beasts; and when he was finished at one place, and had taken the goods that he found in their houses, he betook himself with his followers to another, and dealt there in like manner: by this, such a fear was struck into the Papists that they dared not stay not only in Pravillelm, Viölets, and Bietone <note>These refer to settlements in the Marquisate of Saluzzo, specifically Pravilelm, Biolets, and Bietoné</note>; but also not even in their own dwellings: and they were terrified in such a way that they themselves were the first who brought so much to pass with the Marchioness <note>original: "Marchgravinne" — referring to Margaret of Foix</note> that finally the Believers <note>original: "Geloovige" — here referring to the Waldensian Protestants</note> obtained permission to return to their own, and to possess everything in peace and security.

# WALDENSIANS.

And in such a desired state were those of Pragela <note>original: "Pragillelm" — a valley in the Cottian Alps</note> and others left, until the time that they received the desired news of the Reformation <note>original: "Reformaci"</note> in the Swiss Confederacy <note>original: "Eedt-genootschap"</note> and in Germany.

In the year 1501, the Archbishop of Embrun <note>original: "Ambrun"</note>—who treated the pious people as inhumanly as we have already understood—was not only mockingly addressed by the King’s Envoy, the Official of Orleans, on account of his ignorance; but was also commanded in all earnestness that he should restore the goods which he had extorted from the people under the pretext of religion. This Archbishop was especially vexed that the aforementioned Official had openly given very good testimony to the Waldensians at Embrun, in the Inn of the Angel. But after the King had been well-informed by his Confessor regarding all matters in Dauphiné <note>original: "Delphinat"</note>, he had the following Edict published for the benefit of the Waldensians:

->**Louis, by the grace of God, King of France, etc.**<-

Since we have been informed of what great grievances and oppressions the inhabitants of the Freissinières Valley <note>original: "Dals Fraissiniere"</note> have endured; we therefore wish to come to their aid, so that they may again obtain their goods, namely movable and immovable <term>movable and immovable: a legal distinction referring to personal possessions (movable) versus land and buildings (immovable)</term>. When this preceding Royal Edict was published, the majority of the people said: since the Archbishop possesses the largest and best portion of the Waldensians' goods, it is right that he also make the beginning by surrendering them; for what the others possess, that was given to them as a reward <note>The "others" refers to local officials or neighbors who were granted confiscated lands for assisting in the persecutions</note>. # WALDENSIANS.

Pope Alexander VI <note>Rodrigo Borgia, reigned 1492–1503</note>; but others said that one could accomplish such things with less expense through the Legate, the Cardinal of Saint-Sixtus <note>original: "Sanct Xist"</note>, who was residing in France.

When they had now obtained two special Bulls <term>Bulls: formal proclamations issued by the Pope, named after the lead seal or 'bulla' attached to them</term>, in which indulgence <note>A grant by the Pope of remission of the temporal punishment in purgatory still due for sins after absolution</note> was given for Simony <term>Simony: the buying or selling of ecclesiastical privileges or offices</term>, Theft, Murder, Usury, Fornication, Adultery, Sorcery, Sacrilege <note>original: "Kercken-roverye," literally church-robbery</note>, Perjury, and all kinds of cunning and deceit, public robbery, and apostasy from the faith; yet the Archbishop would not agree to it, but said: one could buy such things from the Pope for money, whose Court was nothing other than a marketplace and a house of profit; and thus he wished to give nothing back to anyone, even though the Indulgence was most abundantly shared with them.

In the year 1506, under King Louis XII, at the instigation of the Prelates and Monks, the Waldensians in Provence were vehemently persecuted.

But although men have attempted to suppress them in all places and to root them out entirely; yet trustworthy Histories testify that around the year 1530, eight hundred thousand Waldensians were still found who held fast to the pure doctrine of the Gospel.

Around the year 1532, the Believers in Piedmont began to hold gatherings to preach and to offer their prayers openly, and they feared no one any longer; but committed themselves to the dear God, with such

<meta>catchword: refo-</meta>

<summary>The Archbishop of Embrun refuses to return Waldensian property despite papal indulgences, citing the corruption of the Roman court; meanwhile, the Waldensian population persists and grows in Provence and Piedmont despite ongoing persecution.</summary>
<keywords>Pope Alexander VI, Cardinal of Saint-Sixtus, Indulgences, King Louis XII, Provence, Piedmont, Waldensians, religious persecution</keywords>

--- Page 86 ---
<meta>This page continues the account of the persecution of the Waldensians in the 1530s. The previous page ended with the believers in Piedmont beginning to hold public gatherings and reaffirming their faith despite the threat of violence.</meta>

# CHRONICLE OF THE

resolution: that they were willing and ready to suffer everything for the Divine truth, and to endure whatever might happen to them according to the providence of God.

As soon as the Bishop of Sisteron <note>original: "Cisteron"</note>, the Abbot of Cavaillon, and others learned of this in the year 1534, they had them sought out and brought into the prisons. And after they learned that a great many of them were from Piedmont, and had investigated the state of affairs in Piedmont through them, they wrote in haste to the Archbishop and to the Inquisitors at Turin <note>original: "Thurin"</note>; who informed Duke Charles <note>Charles III, Duke of Savoy</note> and, through their persistent urging, brought it about that he ordered Pantaleon Bersur to travel into Provence <note>original: "Probancen"</note> to investigate the Piedmontese there and to learn from them the situation of the Waldensians.

After Bersur had received this command, he immediately wrote to the officials <term>Amptluyden: local administrative or judicial officers</term> of the above-mentioned Bishops, and earnestly requested them to delay the trials of the imprisoned Piedmontese in their territory until his arrival.

But this writing of his came too late, for thirteen of the prisoners had already been delivered into the hands of the secular authorities <note>This usually meant they were handed over for execution, as the church "relaxed" heretics to the state for punishment</note>.

Meanwhile, Bersur arrived in Provence and learned so much from the prisoners that he obtained a sufficient knowledge of all the Waldensians who resided within the Duke's territory.

# W A L D E N S I A N S.

With this information he returned again to Piedmont and showed everything to the Inquisitors; they themselves persisted with the Duke until he gave the aforementioned Bersur full power to lay hands upon all the Waldensians.

But in order that he might put his instructions into effect all the better and more hastily, he took five hundred men with him, both on horseback and on foot, and fell with violence upon the borders of Angrogna <note>original: "Angrogne," a key Waldensian valley</note> before the people were aware of it, plundering, robbing, and murdering everyone he encountered. The believers, on the other hand, left their plows and other field-work, and fell upon their enemies with their slings with such a brave and undaunted spirit that they struck down a good many of them, wounded many others, and put the rest to flight, chasing them away and reclaiming the loot.

This proceeding went very much to the heart of the Lord President, the Count of Lucerna <note>original: "Lucerne"</note>, and the widow of the Lord of Angrogna: they wrote an earnest letter of reproach to the aforementioned Bersur regarding this matter, stating that he had thus attacked their subjects without their knowledge, and warned him at the same time not to undertake such a thing again.

What sort of answer followed this writing is not mentioned. But it is noted that Bersur brought about other writings from the Duke, by which the inhabitants in the Valley of Lucerna, Angrogna, and Saint Martin <note>original: "Sanct Martijn"</note> were forbidden to [gather] themselves together with armed hand

But they made their case all the worse thereby; for the King commanded them that they should live according to the ordinances of the Roman Church, or otherwise he would have them punished most severely as heretics. ...forth again, and lived and multiplied there in such a way that, in the year 1618, there were many magnificent Churches all around in Provence.

The enemies of these godly people commonly died a terrible death.

The Advocate Guerin, who had made a false report against them, was hanged.

In the year 1550, the persecutions in Piedmont began in earnest. The Inquisitor <term>Inquisitoor: an official of the Catholic Church tasked with identifying and suppressing heresy</term>, along with the Parliament at Turin, summoned the inhabitants of the Valley of Angrogne—in the person of their Judges—to appear before them, so that they might answer the questions that would be put to them concerning Religion. Now they, to testify to their obedience, sent two from among them; who, despite the promises of safe conduct made to them, were held captive. Meanwhile, those at Angrogne were urged, in the name of the whole people, to send others; but since they had been deceived the first time, they would not undertake this without a certain guarantee of safe passage.

After they had obtained the same, they appointed and dispatched others to press for the release of the other prisoners; but as the unrest of the war in the land increased more and more, this matter was set aside for a time.

In the year 1555, President Saint-Julien, with a fellow Commissioner named Ecclesia, came by the King's command into the Valley of Perouse <note>original: "Dal Perouse"</note>...

Then the delegates of the congregations requested a copy or transcript of this commission and the speech delivered, so that they might show it to all the people and deliberate upon it.

The President refused them this and said they must go to **Mass**; but no one would give him a hearing: saying with one accord that they were not bound to obey such a command, since the same was in conflict with the command of God.

Then he commanded them that some of them—of whom he named forty-six from various Congregations—should betake themselves in haste to *Turin* and allow themselves to be placed in prison there, where they would then hear and await their sentence and judgment; and he thereupon commanded the *Syndic* <term>Syndic: a local government official or magistrate</term> and the chieftains in the Valley that they should dismiss all foreigners and, in the future, suffer no more *Preachers* nor engage any more *Schoolmasters*, except only such as the *Bishop* should send to them.

Because of this, the Parliament became so enraged that they ordered all those whom they could capture to be burned.

While this heavy persecution lasted, the Protesting German Princes, as well as the Reformed Cities of the Confederacy <note>The Swiss Confederacy</note>, made an intercession for the believers, and prayed to King Henry II that he would henceforth allow them to live in peace and quiet in his Kingdom and Lands within their Religion, in which they and their ancestors had now lived for several hundred years. The King promised them that he would keep their intercession in mind; and upon this, the believers remained unmolested until the year 1559, when peace was made between France and Spain, and the Duke of Savoy was reinstated.

Just as with the conclusion of this peace, a marriage was arranged between Duke Emmanuel Philibert and Margaret, the sister of King Henry of France.

# WALDENSIANS.

Nevertheless, the Churches in Piedmont <note>A region in northwest Italy, home to the Waldensian valleys</note> enjoyed good peace for a while longer: for since the Duke was well aware of their faithfulness and sincerity, he did not wish to harm them. Also, the Duke was very much concerned with their well-being.

But because the Pope in Rome, the King in Spain, the Italian Princes, and the Parliaments in Piedmont constantly urged the Duke toward the persecution and extermination of the Reformed <note>original: "Gereformeerde", the Protestant Waldensians</note>; he finally allowed himself to be persuaded, and through a public Mandate, in the year 1560, on the 15th of February, forbade each and every one of his subjects from hearing the Preachers in the Valley of Lucerne and in other places, upon pain of death.

But when they did not heed such threats, he had them attacked with open force; and he entrusted this war to the Count of la Trinité <note>original: "Grave de la Trinité"</note>: alongside this, he also let the monks, Jacomel and Corbis, proceed with the Inquisition.

But after the Count of la Trinité was driven back and beaten, he pulled the fox-skin over the lion-hide <note>A metaphorical expression meaning he swapped brute force for cunning and deception</note>, and acted toward the Believers as if he meant very well by them and had good intentions, and excused both the Duke and himself, as if the soldiery had done much that had not been commanded of them: and he said, he wished

<meta>This page continues the debate among the Waldensian believers regarding the Count of la Trinité’s demand that they dismiss their ministers to appease the Duke of Savoy. The previous page ended with the believers' initial refusal to be persuaded.</meta>

# WALDENSES.

If we allowed our Pastors <note>original: "Zielzorgers" - literally 'soul-carers', the spiritual leaders of the community</note> to be taken from us, we would be following a worldly <note>original: "vleeschelijcken" - literally 'fleshy' or carnal, implying human wisdom rather than divine</note> counsel, upon which a curse hangs. For if our enemies gained this advantage over us—that no one remained to comfort, teach, punish, or exhort us—they would surely apply all their power to ensure that the door remained permanently shut against our Pastors in the future; through whose service we have been so well instructed and strengthened against the temptations of the world.

And since we would be accused of being rebels and disobedient anyway, should we try to recall them later, it is surely better that we keep them now, and not allow ourselves to be robbed of the fruit of their service. It is also better that we be regarded as people who serve God, and who protect those whom God has sent to them to teach and preach to them, etc.

This, and whatever else was said, could nonetheless not prevent the Ministers <note>term: Predicanten - Protestant preachers or pastors</note> from being asked to withdraw for a time and stay nearby in Pragila <note>Pragelato, a neighboring valley then under French control where the Waldensians had many brethren</note> with their fellow believers. But then, one quickly became aware of the judgments of God upon them, and the beginning of all curse and judgment.

There was a great weeping among all the people, and the entire Valley was filled with crying; women and children escorted the Ministers to the summit of the mountain.

And when they saw that the most prominent among them were departing for Turin <note>The capital of the Duchy of Savoy</note>, some said that

this matter did not please them well, and they feared that it would not go well for them; it appeared as if God wished to withdraw His hand from them because they had abandoned their faithful Teachers <note>original: "Leeraers" - the spiritual leaders or pastors of the Waldensian congregation</note>.

And it came to pass, alas! just as those who did not favor the counsels of the flesh <note>original: "raedtslagen des vleesches" - a biblical idiom referring to human wisdom or worldly compromise as opposed to spiritual conviction</note> had foretold: for as soon as they arrived in Turin <note>The capital of the Duchy of Savoy</note>, they were thrown into prison and most strictly confined, and guarded in such a manner that no one was permitted to come to them.

Also, their trial <note>original: "Proces" - legal proceedings, often leading to execution in heresy cases</note> was immediately prepared against them as heretics <note>term: ketters - a person holding beliefs at odds with the established Roman Catholic Church</note>, and all passages and roads were so well guarded that nothing could be heard of them.

Meanwhile, la Trinité <note>The Count of la Trinité, the military commander leading the campaign against the Waldensians</note> told the Believers at Angrogne <note>Angrogna, a key valley and stronghold for the Waldensian community</note> that he had heard that the Duke <note>Emmanuel Philibert, Duke of Savoy</note> had granted their Deputies <note>term: Gedeputeerde - representatives sent to negotiate on behalf of the community</note> everything they had desired, and that he was only detaining them for another purpose: until he had further built up the fortress not far from Angrogne, in order to better secure them according to the promises they had made.

This immediately caused them all kinds of evil thoughts, as they could hear absolutely nothing from their people, and la Trinité would also not allow them to send anyone to Turin.

Finally, after they had diligently reflected upon all these matters and considered the great peril <note>original: "pericul"</note> in which they stood, they unanimously resolved to commend the entire matter to Almighty God, and to...

# THE WALDENSIANS.

->to reconcile with him through fasting and prayer.<-

When they had now done this, they began once more to call upon their Preachers <note>The Waldensian pastors, often called 'Barbes'</note> and to seek help from their neighboring fellow-believers in Pragila <note>Pragelato, a high alpine valley</note>, who immediately came to their aid. They went to the church where la Trinité <note>The Count of la Trinité, the military commander</note> had caused Altars and Images to be erected; they overturned and smashed everything and, as an abomination before God, cast them out. They then stormed the Fortress and defeated the opponents, driving away the entire faction of their enemies and killing a great number of them.

At this, Duke Emmanuel Philibert <note>The Duke of Savoy (1528–1580)</note> became greatly incensed and wished to take revenge upon the prisoners; but the Duchess <note>Margaret of France, Duchess of Savoy, who was known to be sympathetic to the Reformers</note> pleaded for them and stilled the Duke's wrath to the best of her ability. For she pointed out to him the manner in which their Deputies, contrary to the promises made, had been treated and mistreated, and that they saw their own destruction before their eyes and could not have acted otherwise.

Despite this, the Duke was so urged and influenced by the Pope's Legates that he forced the Deputies to attend the Mass <note>term: Misse - the central liturgical ritual of the Roman Catholic Church, which the Waldensians rejected</note> and commanded them to publicly ask forgiveness from God, the Pope, and the Church, with the promise that they would, in the future, live according to their commands and institutions. And after this had occurred, he sent them home again and ordered the Fortress to be rebuilt.

And although the Duchess remained favorable toward the believers, they nevertheless, through the urging

# Chronicle of the

...servants of the Pope and the Monks, one persecution after another had to arise.

In the **Valley of Lucerna** <note>Val Pellice, one of the primary Waldensian valleys</note>, Count William at that same time caused great distress to the Believers of those places. He traveled to Nice, where the Duke was staying at the time, and presented himself to be used earnestly against the believers. And because of this offer, a thousand crowns were ordained, which he was to collect from certain **Reformed congregations**.

And whereas an Edict had been issued in which a fine of one hundred crowns was set upon everyone who should listen to an **Evangelical Preacher**; and where half of the fine was granted to those who could capture a Believer, or at least would discover or reveal them: So this Count, with other Noblemen and their servants, kept diligent watch every day where the Sermons were held upon all those who went in, and noted them down. But although he had recorded many in his roll, and could have received a great sum of money from these same fines, God nevertheless so ordained it that all these fines came to nothing.

In the **Valley of St. Martin** <note>Val Germanasca</note>, there were primarily two Noblemen, Lords over **Rioclaret** <note>Rodoretto</note>, who incited much evil against the Believers: they brought a Bookseller into the **Inquisition** <note>The ecclesiastical court for the suppression of heresy</note> and finally to his death. A Preacher, who had gone early in the morning to one of his Churches to Preach, they...

One of these Noblemen set out at one time, with many armed men, for the Church of **Rioclaret** <note>Rodoretto, a village in the Prali region</note>, while the **Sermon** <note>original: "Predicatie"</note> was being held: he sent some traitors ahead, who acted as if they had come to hear the preaching: they approached as closely as possible to reach the **Pulpit**, in order to prevent the **Preacher** <note>original: "Predicant"</note> from escaping. Meanwhile, the Nobleman arrived there with his men: one of the treacherous villains attacked the Preacher: the listeners resisted this violence of their enemies so manfully that, even though they were unarmed, they rescued the Preacher and put the entire crowd to flight; they could have even slain the Nobleman himself, had they not spared him.

Because of this, he became even more embittered against them, traveled to **Nice**, and urged that the **Reformed** <note>The Waldensians, who had formally joined the Protestant Reformation at the Synod of Chanforan in 1532</note> should be rooted out. This was granted to him, and he was given a hundred men so that he might force his subjects to the **Mass** <note>The Roman Catholic liturgy, which the Waldensians rejected</note>: under this pretext, he gathered a crowd of godless rogues together and led them, before daybreak, toward Rioclaret: As soon as they arrived there, they began to plunder, and to murder man, woman, and child; when the neighbors heard that howling and piteous cry of murder, they rose up and hurried in great fear, and

# CHRONICLE OF THE

For the enemies would not permit them to return to their houses, unless they would promise that they would go to the **Mass** <note>The Roman Catholic liturgy</note>.

A short time before, a **Preacher** <note>original: "Predicant"</note> from **Calabria** <note>A region in Southern Italy where Waldensian colonies had existed since the 14th century</note> had come home again; he took it upon himself to visit and comfort the poor people in the woods; but the enemies seized him and led him to **Pignerol** <note>Pinerolo, a city in Piedmont</note>. There they pressed him hard, but being unable to bring him to **apostasy** <note>original: "af-val"; renouncing his faith</note>, he was—along with another from the **Valley of Saint Martin**—burned in a most gruesome manner.

Meanwhile, the deprivation and the cold increased among those who had fled. After suffering such loss, this Nobleman sought every means to avenge himself; he accused the Believers before the Duke at **Nice**, and desired their complete extirpation; but God put a bit in his mouth: for when he, with other Lords, wished to enjoy himself on that Sea near **Nice**, he was—along with his Brother—captured by a **Turkish Galley** <note>Barbary pirates or Ottoman naval vessels that frequently raided the Mediterranean coast</note>, treated poorly, and only released again for a certain sum of money; which then resulted in him forgetting the Believers for a short time and leaving them in peace.

During this time, those in the **Peruse Valley** <note>Val Perosa</note> had few peaceful or good days.

In particular, the Believers at **Saint Germain** <note>San Germano Chisone</note> had to suffer the cross and persecution heavily; for as this place lay all too near to the **Monks** <note>likely referring to the Abbey of Pinerolo or local monastic orders</note>, they would not suffer at all that the **Reformed Religion** <note>Protestantism</note> be practiced there; they therefore brought together some three hundred godless rogues, whom they sent here and there; these then practiced all kinds of robbery and godlessness, bringing many Believers captive to the Monks, by whom they were then—without further trial—condemned to the fire or to the **Galleys** <note>forced labor as rowers on warships</note>, whenever they refused to embrace the **Roman Religion**.

And because the **Preacher** <note>original: "Predicant"</note> at **Saint Germain** was to the above-mentioned Monks just like a

# Chronicle of the

thorn in their side, they appointed a traitor—who knew the Preacher well—who, with a murderous mob, positioned himself before the Preacher's house a little before daybreak. Since the Preacher recognized the traitor's voice and suspected no evil from him, he went out with him at his request; but he immediately noticed the treachery and tried to save himself by flight, crying out for help; however, he was overtaken, wounded, and taken prisoner. Many inhabitants of that place, when they wished to come to the aid of their Pastor <note>original: "Ziel-sorger," literally "Soul-carer"</note>, were some wounded, some slain, and others carried away captive as well.

The houses were plundered, and the loot was brought to the Monks: where also the Preacher—after he had endured many torments and temptations <note>likely referring to pressures to recant his faith</note>—was finally burned: to which fire some women of St. Germain, who were also imprisoned for the sake of the faith, were forced to carry the wood.

The same soldiers employed similar tyranny in several other places located nearby: in such a manner that many wealthy people, who previously were able to come to the aid of other needy persons with their goods, were brought to extreme poverty and entirely to the beggar's staff <note>an idiom meaning to be reduced to total destitution</note>.

Nevertheless, God frequently allowed the enemies to be struck by the believers in such a way that they finally became weary themselves and laid down their weapons.

--- Page 107 ---
<meta>This page continues the history of the Waldensian persecutions, shifting focus from the martyrdom in Saint Germain to the diplomatic and military pressures placed upon the inhabitants of the Lucerna Valley in 1560.</meta>

# WALDENSIANS.

After this, the Commissioner Corbis issued an order to those in the Lucerna Valley <note>The Val Lucerna, one of the three primary Waldensian valleys in the Cottian Alps</note> that they must attend the Mass and subject themselves to all ordinances of the Roman Church, upon pain of death.

The response of the Reformed was briefly this: Their religion was consistent with the Holy Scripture, the Faith of the Apostles, and the Doctrine of the early Christian Church. If anyone could convict them of any error using the Holy Scripture, they were willing and ready to abandon it. If it could also be proven to them from the Holy Scripture that the Mass and other similar institutions of the Roman Church were holy and good, they would gladly accept <note>original: "aennemem," likely a printer's error for "aennemen"</note> them.

But if this could not be shown to them, they earnestly desired that they might be left in peace and quiet with their religion.

Following this answer, they remained unmolested for several days. But in April of this year, 1560, the Lord of Raconis came to Saint John <note>San Giovanni, a Waldensian parish</note> and made his presence known at a sermon on a Wednesday. After the sermon was finished, he spoke much of the Duke’s <note>Duke Emmanuel Philibert of Savoy</note> leniency and testified that the fact they had been so violently persecuted had not happened with his prior knowledge. Nevertheless, one had to consider the means to pacify His Highness; he therefore asked the believers what they thought would be most useful for this purpose. And even though they would have much preferred to see

# Chronicle of the

that the Lord of Raconis <note>M. de Raconis, a representative of the Duke of Savoy</note> should have first revealed his own opinion regarding this; they nevertheless felt compelled to do the same. And this was then their answer: that they considered the best means to be the submission of their humble petition to His Highness <note>Duke Emmanuel Philibert</note>, alongside their Confession of Faith and a written defense against all kinds of false accusations; since, in such circumstances, the early Christians were accustomed to using similar means. This pleased the Lord of Raconis well, and they therefore gave him such writings to be delivered to the Duke; and so it appeared that this persecution might be somewhat quieted.

Immediately following this, the Lord of Raconis, together with the Count of La Trinité <note>The Count of La Trinité was a military commander known for his later harshness toward the Waldensians</note>, returned to the Valley of Lucerna and summoned all the Ministers and Syndics <note>term: Sindicus/Cindicis: local civil magistrates or community representatives</note> before him. He informed them that he had come to them for the purpose of quieting the persecution. And after they had both highly praised the Duke’s great friendliness, they questioned the Ministers on several Articles of their Religion, and especially concerning the calling of the Ministers <note>The legitimacy of their ordination outside the Roman Church</note> and the obedience owed to the Authorities; to which the Ministers of the Church also gave their answers.

Thereafter, these Lords said that their writings had been delivered to the Duke, and that he intended to send them to the Pope. They also asked the Syndics whether they would be willing to hear the preachers whom the Duke might send to them

They answered / that they could not resolve to silence their Ministers / until they were first assured / that those whom they wished to give them / were Servants of God / and Ministers of the pure Evangelical Doctrine <note>The Waldensians identified their faith as the "pure" Gospel in contrast to Roman Catholic traditions</note>. When the above-mentioned defense was read before the Duke and his Council / some / including the Duke himself / were of the opinion / that it was not just that such people should be so persecuted / who were willing to accept everything / that corresponds with God's Word; and that it was therefore necessary / that they should be convinced of their errors. Others, on the contrary, said / that one should not act or decide in such matters / without the prior knowledge of the Pope / and that it was therefore necessary / that these writings be sent to Rome / which was also done.

# CHRONICLE OF THE 92

but he desired that they all, and each one in particular, should submit themselves to the institutions of the Roman Church. He also said that, since gentle means had until now been unable to help these people, it would be most certain that one should take a firm hand and persecute them with violence.

When this answer had come from Rome, it was resolved in the Duke’s Council to follow the Pope’s opinion. However, so that their tyranny might have a better appearance, they did not wish to take up arms immediately in the beginning; but they first sent out a certain Commissioner, named Possevino <note>original: "Paussevin," referring to Antonio Possevino, a Jesuit diplomat and polemicist</note>, into the Valleys <note>The Waldensian Valleys in the Piedmont region</note>, who was intended on one hand to dismiss the Ministers, and on the other hand to bring the people to apostasy through all kinds of cunning and deceit. But since this means also proved largely fruitless, the aforementioned Possevino returned to the Court of the Duke in the month of September <note>original: "Herfst-Maent," literally "Harvest-month"</note>, and through his manifold false testimonies, he brought it about that it was decided to attack the Reformed <note>term: Gereformeerde: a term used here for the Waldensians who had aligned with the Protestant Reformation</note> in the Valleys with armed force.

When the Believers understood this decision, they petitioned the Duke—partly through others and partly by themselves—for gracious pardon and for the freedom of their true Religion.

# WALDENSIANS.

Count Charles, in the Valley of Lucerna <note>original: "Dal Lucerne," a primary Waldensian valley in Piedmont</note>, exhorted them—especially those at Angrogna <note>original: "Angrogne," a key Waldensian stronghold</note>—that they should accommodate themselves somewhat to the times, by dismissing their Ministers <note>term: Predicanten: Protestant clergy or preachers</note> and accepting those who would be sent to them. But when they would not do so, he made another request, desiring that they should at least keep their Ministers somewhat hidden, and then say that they had fled away. He also wished to have a Mass sung at Angrogna, to which they would not be forced in the least; but this cunning trickery was also in vain.

When the Believers now saw that things were being arranged for an open persecution, their Ministers joined together on several occasions and deliberated with one another about the matter, so that they might prevent their total destruction. After they had decided together, in the fear of God, to do nothing at all that might be detrimental to the honor of God or contrary to His Holy Word, they began to exhort all their people with the highest earnestness: that they should, through true repentance and faith, as well as through fervent prayers, take their refuge in God; that they should provide themselves with all necessities, and bring their household goods and the weak persons into safety in the highest reaches of the nearest mountains. And when finally, in the month of October <note>original: "Wijn-maent," literally "Wine-month"</note>, the enemy was on the march,

# CHRONICLE OF THE

they held a general Day of Fasting and Prayer, and on the following Sunday, the Holy Supper <note>term: Heyligh Abontmael: The Protestant sacrament of Communion or the Lord's Supper</note>.

Thereupon they were joyful in the Lord their God: for they had made themselves entirely cheerful for the approaching struggle through prayers and the singing of Psalms.

Immediately after this, the battle began; for on the last day of October <note>original: "Wijnmaent," the traditional Dutch name for the month of wine harvest</note>, Angrogna <note>A central village and mountain stronghold for the Waldensian community</note> was persecuted with fire and sword. The next day, the Count de la Trinité <note>The commander of the Duke of Savoy's forces</note> arrived with his army and encamped at Bobbio Pellice <note>original: "Bubiane"</note>: his vanguard made a raid into the villages of San Giovanni <note>original: "St. Joan"</note> and plundered everything, also taking some prisoners; but when the inhabitants of those places likewise took up arms, the enemies sought better air <note>A Dutch idiom meaning they fled or retreated to a safer location</note>.

On the second day of December <note>original: "Wintermaent," the traditional Dutch name for the month of winter</note>, the enemies came toward Angrogna; the Believers, who had not expected such an assault, hastily set their watch. And although the same consisted of only a hundred persons, they had nevertheless resolved to give their lives for their Religion and their own; they also fell upon their knees in the presence of the enemies and prayed earnestly to God.

The Count de la Trinité advanced upon them with his people: here the watch stood against them, retreating gradually toward the heights, and thus escaped the enemy. The Count, not wishing to wait for any more such maneuvers, drew back; but in the retreat toward San Giovanni and Angrogna, he reduced many houses to ashes.

Afterward, he came to La Torre <note>original: "a la Cour," likely referring to La Torre Pellice, the administrative center of the valleys</note>, in a beautiful—

# THE WALDENSIANS.

village lying in the Valley of Lucerna, on a level plain, and he acted there most cruelly; he did the same in another place, but with little advantage.

When the Count now saw that his designs would not succeed, and that the Reformed <note>The Waldensians, who had formally aligned with the Protestant Reformation</note> had resolved to risk themselves to the last man, he changed his mind and wished to use cunning instead of force. He gave those at Angrogna good words and offered them a fair agreement. But while still occupied with this, he marched with his people on the fourth of December <note>original: "Wintermaent"</note> against Taillaire and Villar. The party that had marched against Villar sought to climb to the heights in a large village. But when the appointed watch saw them, they—after they had offered their prayer—set themselves against the enemies with such manliness that they killed some of them, wounded others, and put the rest to flight. After this had happened, they came to the aid of those at Taillaire, where the battle lasted a long time and very many of the enemies were slain; but of the believers, not one was killed—only three who were slightly wounded, yet they were very quickly healed again.

The Count wished, so it appeared from the outside, to make peace again; he requested four of the most prominent men of Angrogna to negotiate with them. These men, after their return, said that they had been friendly received by the Count, and that he tes-

### CHRONICLE OF THE...

...had testified <note>Completing the word "getuyght" from the previous page.</note> that when he was about to depart from the **Duke’s** Court, the **Duke** said to him: that even if the **Pope**, the **Italian princes and cities**, and indeed his own **Council** had decided to completely exterminate <term>original: "uyt te roepen," meaning to proscribe, root out, or announce the destruction of a group</term> these people, the Lord God had nevertheless put a different counsel into his heart—namely, that he should treat these people kindly and with friendship; and many other such things which the **Count** had said.

And while the people allowed themselves to be delayed by such a false pretense, news arrived from **Angrogna** that a portion of the army had crossed the mountain lying between **Angrogna** and **la Cour** and had arrived near **Angrogna**, having burned everything in their path with fire. Some men were dispatched against them, by whom the attackers were beaten back; and though the enemy was numerous, they were scattered without the loss of a single man among the [believers].

But because the **Count** still constantly pressed for a treaty, those of **Angrogna** finally decided among themselves to dispatch several men to the **Duke** at **Vercelli** <note>original: "Verceil," modern-day Vercelli in the Piedmont region of Italy.</note>, and to request of him, by means of a **Petition** <term>original: "Supplicatie," a formal, humble written request to a sovereign</term>, that he would allow them to live in safety according to their religion; in return, they would show him all dutiful obedience at all times, as they had done until now.

For this reason, the most prominent men, not only from **Angrogna** but also from the other communities of the **Lucerna Valley**, were [sent] to the **Duke** with

...times <note>Completing the sentence from the previous page: "at all times."</note> the **Petition** <term>original: "Supplicatie," a formal humble request</term> was dispatched, accompanied by **Gastaut**, a Secretary.

Once the cunning fox, the **Count of la Trinité**, had lured out a great number of the most prominent men from the Valleys, and thus deprived the poor people of their best advisors, he began to put his evil plan into action, starting first against those of **Taillaire** <note>Taillaire likely refers to the inhabitants of the Tagliante region in the Piedmontese Alps.</note>.

He accused them of not gathering quickly enough to negotiate the agreement; and that they had also let slip threatening words against his soldiers. He refused to hear any excuses, but ordered them to bring all their weapons and, on account of their "crimes," to beg for mercy on their knees; some did this out of great fear, but others did not.

Shortly thereafter, he attacked the **Taillaire**, plundered everything, and what he could not carry away, he burned.

Again, he pretended to want to deal with them in kindness: he commanded them to gather at a certain place for that purpose and answer him whether they would surrender their own <note>Likely referring to surrendering their religious ministers or their means of defense.</note> and live in peace. Because of this, they gathered by night in a village that was difficult to reach. And when day broke, they saw that they were surrounded on all sides by the enemy; nevertheless, they were all able to escape, though with great peril, and evade the violence.

## Chronicle of the...
98

The violence of the enemies, which was closest to **La Cour** <note>Likely referring to La Torre Pellice, a central town in the Waldensian valleys, or a specific fortified manor known as "the Court."</note>, caused a great terror among the inhabitants of that place. And since they did not dare to openly take up arms, nor seek help from their neighbors (for their envoys were with the **Duke** <term>original: "Hertoch," referring to Emmanuel Philibert, Duke of Savoy</term> and were hoping for a merciful answer), they resolved to leave their houses and save their lives. To that end, some retreated to the high mountains into the caves, even though it was the middle of winter; others fled elsewhere. When the **Soldiers** <note>The troops under the command of the Count of la Trinité.</note> learned of this, they plundered everything that was still to be found, and even those hidden on the mountains were not safe from them.

In a certain cave, they found a mother with her daughters; these they wounded unto death. In another cave, they found a man who was one hundred and three years old, together with his granddaughter. They killed the man; they intended to violate the daughter, but she threw herself down from the **rocky cliff** <term>original: "steenrotze"</term> and died.

When the godless soldiers had finished plundering that place, they went into the community of **Villar** <note>Villar Pellice, a village further up the valley from La Torre.</note>. There, they likewise plundered everything, taking men and women away in bonds. These captives were forced to carry the very loot and drive the cattle that had been stolen from them. The poor prisoners were severely mistreated by them: one maddened villain bit off a man's ear, saying he wanted to carry **Heretics'** <term>original: "Ketters," a derogatory term used by the Catholic forces for the Waldensian Protestants</term> flesh into his own country.

-> # W A L D E N S E N. <-

After all these horrible acts of tyranny had been committed, the **Count** <note>The Count of la Trinité, the military commander overseeing the persecution.</note> demanded twenty thousand **crowns** <term>original: "kroonen," a gold coin used across Europe</term> from these poor people to pay the **Soldiers**, so that they might be dismissed; but upon their persistence, he reduced the amount by four thousand; and the **Duke** <note>Emmanuel Philibert, Duke of Savoy.</note> reduced it by half again <note>Reducing the total demand from 20,000 to 8,000 crowns.</note>. However, because there had previously been a great **famine** <term>original: "duure tijdt," literally a "dear" or expensive time</term>, and because these good people had otherwise been frequently robbed of their means, they could only bring this sum together with great difficulty. Now, the eight thousand crowns were raised and paid, but the Soldiers had to remain just the same.

The Count could still not be satisfied with this, but commanded that all **weapons** belonging to the **Reformed** <term>original: "Gereformeerde," referring to the Waldensian Protestants</term> should be delivered to him. He also desired the eight thousand crowns which the Count <note>The text suggests the Count retracted his earlier debt forgiveness, demanding the full original sum.</note> had previously remitted to them. He commanded all **Ministers** <term>original: "Predikanten"</term> to depart until the Duke had revealed his intentions. It was therefore decided that they should withdraw for a short time until the Army had broken camp and moved away; and they were to be escorted to the Valleys of **St. Martin** and **Pragela** <note>Val San Martino and Pragelato, neighboring Waldensian valleys.</note>. But this did not happen without great damage to them: for when the Soldiers had learned of the Ministers’ journey, and that they were together at **Puy** <note>A village in the valley of San Martino.</note>, they followed them; but when they did not find them there, they returned to the nearest Villages, where they, under the pretense that

...they searched for the hidden **Ministers** <term>original: "Predikanten"</term>; all chests and cupboards, cellars and rooms, and everything that was locked had to be opened, from which they then took everything that pleased them and departed.

He [the Count] often sent his greetings and requested that he [Noël] might speak with him secretly concerning several points of faith. This he had promised him, with the prior knowledge of his **Congregation** <term>original: "Gemeente"</term>. The conference was held without danger eight days before the departure of the Ministers. At this conversation, the Count appeared with many of his attendants, having left many others nearby in a hidden place. The Count presented three points to the Minister; namely, the **Supremacy of the Pope** <term>original: "Hoogheyt des Paus"</term>, the **Mass**, and the **transformation of the bread into the Body of Christ** <note>The doctrine of Transubstantiation, a central theological dispute where Catholics believe the bread literally becomes the body of Jesus, while the Reformed tradition viewed it symbolically.</note>. To this the Minister answered that he wished to speak of this with the other Ministers and with the Congregation to take counsel, and would then answer his request. The Count was satisfied with this;

### WALDENSIANS.

...and so they departed from one another. A few days later, before **Mr. Noël** <note>Pastor Steffen Noël of Angrogne.</note> had received an answer, the **Count** <note>The Count of la Trinité, the military commander overseeing the persecution.</note> sent his men to **Angrogne**, to **St. Lawrence’s Church**, as if he intended to have a **Mass** <term>original: "Misse"</term> celebrated there. Shortly thereafter, the entire force arrived, searched and plundered everything, and mistreated many poor people, demanding to know from them where the Minister was. When they learned that he was hidden in the mountains and could not be captured, they plundered his house completely and brought his books and writings in sacks to the **General**, who ordered them all to be burned together.

On that same day, the soldiers completely plundered about forty houses within Angrogne, broke the mills to pieces, and carried away the flour. During the night, they searched for Mr. Noël again; but when they did not find him, the **Elders** <term>original: "oudtsten," the lay leaders of the Waldensian congregation</term> were ordered the following day to surrender the Minister. If they failed to do so, Angrogne would be destroyed by **fire and sword** <note>A standard early modern phrase for total military devastation.</note>. The elders replied that they could not do such a thing, as he had been driven away by the soldiers, and they did not know where he might have hidden himself.

Thereupon, the Count was ordered to lead his men into other quarters outside the **Valleys** <note>The Piedmontese valleys where the Waldensian communities were concentrated.</note>.

And although he did so, he nevertheless left a strong **garrison** <term>original: "besettinge"</term> in the **Valleys** <note>The Waldensian Valleys of Piedmont.</note>, through whose wantonness the poor inhabitants suffered great distress.

Of which we wish to recount only two examples, from which it can easily be inferred how the barbaric soldiers conducted themselves toward the poor people. From the village of **Bonnets**, they took two prisoners to the fortress of **la Cour** <note>Likely the fortress at La Torre Pellice, the military headquarters in the region.</note>; and after they had terribly mistreated them, the **Captain** <term>original: "Hooftman"</term> of the garrison himself laid hands on one and strangled him; the other was tortured in a completely gruesome manner by these bloodthirsty rogues: they tied him to a table and placed **horse beetles** <note>original: "paerts Kevers," black beetles that typically live in horse manure</note> on his navel under a saucer, which gnawed at him until they had forced their way into his belly, so that the pious man, in the sixtieth year of his age, died most miserably.

And these were the manifold oppressions and persecutions that the Believers endured in this year of **1560** in the **Piedmontese Valleys**.

In other places, the enemies of the truth were no less sparing in this same year; especially in **Calabria** <note>A region in Southern Italy where Waldensian colonies had existed since the 14th century.</note>; for there the Believers were very heavily oppressed: because when they had obtained **Mr. Louis Pascal** and **Stephen Negrin** <note>Ludovico Paschali and Stefano Negrino, Waldensian pastors sent from Geneva to support the Calabrian communities.</note> as **Ministers** from the Church of **Geneva**, and began to preach the **Gospel** <term>original: "Evangelium"</term> openly, **Pope Pius IV** called the **Cardinals** together; by whom it was decided

it was ordered to denounce all those who, so near the **Holy See** <note>The papal court in Rome.</note>, had dared to bring the **Lutheran doctrine** <note>A general term used by the authorities at the time to refer to Protestant or Reformed teachings, though these communities were specifically Waldensian.</note> into the open. They knew well that they had obtained some teachers from **Geneva**; these they were to abandon and, in the future, live according to the institution of the **Roman Church**; and doing so, they would have nothing to fear. But if they should secretly harbor such teachers, they would put themselves in peril of life and limb, as those who would be condemned as **heretics** <term>original: "Ketters"</term>.

And to know which of them had already departed from the Roman Church, they had the bells rung for **Mass** <term>original: "Misse"</term> and commanded the people to go inside. But instead of hearing the Mass, they fled with their wives and children into the **woods** <note>original: "Bosschagien," referring to the thickets or forested areas surrounding the town.</note>, and no one remained at home except those who, because of weakness or old age, were unable to flee.

Xist"</note>; instead, they hurried to the small town of **La Garde** <note>Guardia Piemontese, a Waldensian stronghold in Calabria.</note>. They promised that if the people of La Garde would do the same, no harm would come to them. Those poor, simple people believed the lying **Monks** and did everything they wanted.

But when they later learned that their fellow believers had actually refused to attend the Mass, they repented in their hearts and were deeply ashamed of their **apostasy** <term>The abandonment or renunciation of a religious belief.</term>. They considered fleeing with their wives and children as well. However, their Lord, **Salvator Spinello** <note>Salvatore Spinelli, the local feudal lord of Guardia Piemontese.</note>, would not allow it. He promised to protect them against anyone who might attack them, provided they remained loyal to the **Roman Church**. In the meantime, the Monks brought two **companies** <note>original: "vaendelen", literally banners, referring to military units.</note> of soldiers against the people of San Sisto. These soldiers rushed toward the poor people as if they were **wild game** <note>original: "wilt-braet".</note>, shouting "*Kill, Kill!*" <note>original: "Amazza, Amazza" (Italian).</note>; and in this manner, they cut down and **strangled** <note>original: "verworght".</note> many of the believers.

<meta>This page continues the narrative of the Waldensian persecution in Calabria in 1560. <header>WALDENSIANS. 105</header>

They begged them to have mercy on themselves, their wives, and their children, as they and their ancestors had lived in the land for several hundred years and had behaved in such a way that no one could complain about them. And if the authorities were no longer willing to tolerate or suffer them in the land, they asked that they at least be allowed to take some of their meager belongings with them. They would then commend themselves to God and depart with their wives and children, either by ship across the sea or by land, promising that they would never return to their homes in that place. They would rather leave everything behind than sin through **idolatry** <term>The worship of idols; here referring to the Roman Catholic rituals the Waldensians rejected as unscriptural.</term>. They begged to be allowed to leave the country for God’s sake, and not to be forced into **defense** <note>original: "defenci"</note> or resistance; for if they could expect no mercy, they would be forced to resort to the extreme <note>This implies they would be forced to fight for their lives if no peaceful exit was permitted.</note>.

But the soldiers became all the more enraged at this and rushed toward them. The **Believers** <note>The author's term for the Waldensians.</note>, with God's help, defended themselves so bravely that they defeated the majority of the soldiers and drove the rest into flight.

Thereupon the **Monks** wrote to the **Viceroy of Naples** <note>The Spanish representative who governed Southern Italy.</note>, who hastily sent two **companies of soldiers** <note>original: "Vaendelen knechten", literally "banners of soldiers," referring to military units.</note> to capture the Believers from **San Sisto** <note>original: "S. Sixt"</note> and **La Garde** who had fled into the forests. By doing so, they would perform a particularly pleasing service for the **Pope** and make themselves meritorious to the **Church**, if they could rid it of such people.

The **Viceroy** <note>The representative of the Spanish King in Naples, who held supreme military and civil authority.</note> also arrived in person with some troops, and had it proclaimed by trumpeters at **San Sisto** <note>original: "S. Xist"</note> that everything would be ravaged with fire and sword, for nothing but blood and fire was to be seen.

In the meantime, the women had brought food to San Sisto to sustain their husbands and children in the forests. The Viceroy had mercy proclaimed to all **bandits** <note>The authorities offered amnesty to criminals and outlaws in exchange for their help in hunting down the religious refugees.</note> throughout the entire Kingdom of Naples, summoning them against the **Believers** <note>The author’s term for the Waldensians.</note>; he intended for them to be used against the same as they were the most harmful **heretics** <term>A person holding religious beliefs at odds with established Roman Catholic dogma; a charge that carried the death penalty.</term>. Thus, a great multitude of all sorts of villains gathered, who were led into the forests against the fled and scattered poor people. And because many had hidden themselves in the thick thorn-bushes, large hunting dogs were brought along to seek them out and force them into the open. There, a gruesome murdering, slaughtering, and killing took place; many hid themselves on the highest peaks of the stone cliffs, and many died of hunger.

The **Monks** behaved as if they took no pleasure in this; and when they encountered the **Syndic** <note>An elected representative or chief magistrate of a town.</note> of San Sisto at **Cosenza** <note>original: "Cossence"</note>, they advised him to make a hasty escape, so that the Viceroy would not find out and have him seized.

By these means, the Believers at **Guardia** <note>Referring to Guardia Piemontese, another Waldensian settlement.</note> were also deceived, who—at the demand of the Monks—[went] to Cosenza and to **Fuscaldo** <note>original: "Foscalda"</note> before the

<page-num>107</page-num>
<header>W A L D E N S I A N S.</header>

the **Viceroy** appeared; for as soon as they had arrived at ***Foscalda*** <note>Modern-day Fuscaldo in Calabria, Italy.</note>, seventy of them were taken prisoner and brought to ***Montalt*** <note>Montalto Uffugo.</note> before the **Inquisitor Pantza** <note>Giulio Panza, a notorious commissioner sent to suppress the Waldensians in Calabria.</note>, who put them all together on the **rack** <term>A torture device consisting of a rectangular frame with rollers at both ends; the victim’s limbs were tied and pulled to induce intense pain and joint dislocation.</term>.

Among others, they had one named ***Steffen Carlijn*** <note>original: "Steffen Carlijn"</note> so hideously tortured that his intestines fell out of his body onto the earth. The Inquisitor intended to force them through such terrible torments to confess to shameful things and to say "yes" to his questions—things they had never thought of in all their lives—only to make them all the more hated among the common people. When the Inquisitors captured beautiful young daughters among the prisoners, they never allowed them to appear in public again; just as at that time nine beautiful young daughters were lost, of whom no news could ever be heard again.

How hideously many were tortured and yet remained steadfast, we shall learn in the sixth Chapter.

After this, the Inquisitors moved to ***S. Agata*** <note>Sant'Agata di Esaro.</note>, where they also delivered a great multitude to the **Secular Authority** <note>The civil government; the Church would "hand over" convicted heretics to the state to carry out the death penalty, as the Church officially avoided shedding blood.</note>.

And if anyone wished to speak a good word on their behalf, they were also immediately put on the rack as someone who desired to defend the **Heretics** <term>Those whose religious beliefs contradicted official Catholic dogma; in this period, heresy was considered a capital crime against both Church and State.</term>, so that no one dared to speak another word.

<meta>This page continues the narrative of the persecution of the Waldensians. The previous page detailed the torture of refugees in Calabria under Inquisitor Panza. This page concludes the Calabrian account and shifts the focus to the escalation of conflict in the Piedmontese valleys in early 1561.</meta>

<page-num>108</page-num>
<header>Chronicle of the</header>

**Pope Pius IV** sent the **Marquis of Buccianico** <note>original: "March-graef van Butiane"; Giovan Girolamo Castiglione, the military commander tasked with the suppression of the Waldensians in Calabria.</note> with such force to completely **root out** <note>original: "uyt te roepen"; to exterminate or drive out entirely.</note> the Believers, with the promise that his son would receive a **Cardinal’s hat** as an honor if he could cleanse **Calabria** of such people. This was easily done by him, as the **Inquisitors** killed almost everyone they could catch; the strong were sent to **Spain** to serve on the **galleys** <term>Large, oar-powered warships where prisoners were chained to benches and forced to row in brutal conditions, often until death.</term>, while the women and children were sold or strangled.

Now that we have heard at length about the horrific persecutions of the year 1560, we shall now proceed to the following times.

When, in the year 1561, at the beginning of January, those who had been appointed by the Believers and sent to the **Duke** <note>Emanuel Philibert, Duke of Savoy.</note> at **Vercelli** <note>original: "Verceil"</note> arrived back in the **Luserna Valley** <note>original: "Dal Lucerne"; one of the primary Waldensian Valleys in the Piedmont region.</note>, there was hope of hearing good news through them, but in vain: for they brought word that they had not only failed to obtain any concessions, but that even harsher decrees had been issued, namely to force them into the **Roman Religion** <note>The Roman Catholic Church.</note> or to root them out entirely. Monks and priests had been ordered to come into the Valleys; also, the force intended to be used against them was so great that it would be impossible to resist.

Because of these unexpected tidings, the Believers became deeply distressed: and to this was added that the **Preachers** <note>original: "Predikanten"; the spiritual leaders of the Waldensian communities.</note> in **Piedmont** <note>The region in northwest Italy bordering France.</note>,

<page-num>109</page-num>
<header>WALDENSIANS.</header>

...had received writings from other distant **Teachers** <note>original: "Leeraers"; these were the ministers or "Barbes" who provided spiritual guidance to the Waldensian communities.</note>, by which they were exhorted to steadfastness: that they should rely on God alone and expect no help from men. This was especially urgent because **Francis II, the King of France**, at the instigation of the **Duke of Guise** and the **Cardinal of Lorraine** <note>Charles of Lorraine, a powerful Catholic prelate who, along with his brother the Duke of Guise, heavily influenced the young King Francis II to take a hard line against the Huguenots and Waldensians.</note>, had resolved to **root out** <note>original: "uyt te roepen"; to exterminate or completely drive out.</note> all of the **Reformed**, but especially those in **Dauphiné** <note>original: "Delphinat"; a province in southeastern France bordering the Italian Alps.</note>.

Although the Lord God removed **Francis, the King of France**, by death <note>Francis II died in December 1560 at the age of sixteen, leading to a temporary shift in French policy under the regency of Catherine de' Medici.</note>, and it seemed as if the rooting out of the **Reformed** would come to nothing, the Believers in **Piedmont** and **Dauphiné** nevertheless bound themselves together anew to remain with one another in the **true Religion** and to faithfully stand by each other in times of need.

When, shortly thereafter, some soldiers arrived to plunder **Bobbio** <note>Bobbio Pellice, one of the primary Waldensian villages in the Luserna Valley.</note> and other places and to take the Believers captive, the **Reformed** joined together and drove them back into their fortress at **Villar** <note>Villar Pellice, a neighboring village with a fortified position.</note>. They besieged them there and demanded the release of the prisoners. The next day, those from the **Castle of La Torre** <note>original: "Casteel la Tour"; the main fortress at Torre Pellice, used as a base for military operations against the valleys.</note> attempted to relieve the besieged; but the appointed guard defeated a part of them and put the remainder to flight.

siege, the Governor of **la Coursels** arrives with his people, and many others, wishing to come to the aid of the besieged by force: but since a portion of his men were slain, and another portion miserably wounded, he was, like the others before him, forced to retreat. Not one of the **Reformed** <note>The "Gereformeerde" refers to the Waldensian believers who had aligned themselves with the Protestant Reformation.</note> was harmed or killed that day.

Those who were now besieging the **Fortress**, though they had no heavy artillery, nonetheless caused so much distress to those within the **Fortress** that they desired to come to terms <note>To "accord" or negotiate a surrender.</note> and depart from there. But the **Fortress** could not be surrendered quickly enough before the **Count de la Trinité** <note>The Count of Trinity was the primary military commander appointed by the Duke of Savoy to suppress the Waldensians.</note> arrives in the **Luserna Valley**.

This arrival of the **Count** caused a great fright among the **Reformed**: yet they strengthened themselves in God, confirmed their newly formed union, and established good order; they commissioned a hundred well-experienced **arquebusiers** <note>original: "Haeck-schutten"; soldiers armed with an arquebus, an early type of long-gun supported by a hook or rest.</note>, so that they would always be ready to come to the aid of those under attack; two **Ministers** <note>original: "Predikanten"; Waldensian spiritual leaders who accompanied the soldiers to provide moral guidance and lead prayer.</note> were also assigned to them, to prevent any disorder, and especially to diligently attend to prayer.

Meanwhile, the **Count** arrives with his people between **San Giovanni** <note>original: "St. Ioan"; a town in the Waldensian valleys.</note> and **Angrogna**, to launch an attack on the watch stationed there.

They fought bravely until midnight, which also brought an end to the battle, in which the **Count** lost many men.

When the entire force of the enemy intended to launch an attack on the **Reformed**, and for that purpose had come down over the rocky cliffs into the valley, twenty-five soldiers stood against this great multitude and bore themselves very manfully; immediately thereafter, the hundred **arquebusiers** <note>Soldiers armed with a "Haeck-schut" or arquebus, an early heavy matchlock firearm.</note> arrived, called upon God with the others, ran to the aid of those who needed their assistance most, and struck such terror into the enemy that they sought to save themselves by flight. ### CHRONICLE OF THE WALDENSIANS | 112

...his own sword, which was four fingers wide, was drawn, and with it his head was struck off: and this was the end of this <term>Captain</term> <note>original: *Hooft-man*; a military officer or commander</note>. Another Captain had already reached the mountains in the clearing of **St. Martin**, attempting to flee; but a young fellow of eighteen years overtook him in the snow and slew him.

The defeat of the enemies would have been much greater if the <term>Minister</term> <note>original: *Predicant*; the spiritual leaders of the Waldensians often accompanied the men into battle to lead prayers and provide moral guidance.</note>, who was assigned to the hundred <term>Arquebusiers</term> <note>original: *Haeck-schutten*; soldiers armed with early matchlock firearms.</note>, had not held back the pursuers: for when he saw that the enemies no longer defended themselves, but only sought to save their lives, he commanded that they should all fall upon their knees, thank God, and shed no more blood. All those who heard this command were obedient, but the others, who knew nothing of it, pursued the enemy until nightfall. And it was held as certain that, if they had all followed the enemy together, not a single one of them would have escaped.

After this, the **Count de la Trinité** turned his attention to **Rora**, a small valley and nearby community in which everyone was devoted to the **Reformed Religion**, though there were no more than 82 households.

For the first two days, he sent no more than a portion of his people against them; but these were driven back. The next day, he let the entire army advance upon them. And because they were too weak to withstand such a great force, they sought their

With this strong army he attacked ***Pra del Tor*** <note>original: *la præ de Tour*; a natural mountain stronghold in the Angrogna valley, central to Waldensian resistance.</note>, located in the valley of ***Angrogne***; in particular, a bulwark—in which only five of the **Reformed** <note>original: *Gereformeerde*; members of the Protestant faith.</note> were present to guard it—was heavily assaulted: of the five, one was killed; the remaining four threw aside their weapons and held off the enemy with stone-throwing long enough until the hundred Arquebusiers came to their aid, who then caused a great bloodbath and slew many of the enemies.

### CHRONICLE OF THE

took leave; he intended to perform horrific deeds that day; but his Landlady <note>original: *Waerdinne*; the mistress of the inn.</note> said to him,
> Lord, if our religion is better than those people's, then you shall retain the victory.

This man was now shot through both his legs in the battle: and when he was carried back into that same inn in the evening, the Landlady said to him,
> Did I not tell you, if their religion is better, that you then would not retain the victory?

When even more men arrived to join the others for the defense <note>original: *verdiffenderinge*</note> of the aforementioned bulwark <term>Bulwark (Bolwerck): A defensive wall or earthwork fortification.</term>, they caused such great damage to the enemy that the Count de la Trinité is said to have wept because of the high number of the slain.

There was also another bulwark on the heights of the mountain, which the enemies also approached; those who were inside only began their defense when the enemies were close by: there, once again, through gunfire, large stones, and other means, so many of the enemy were laid low that they had to retreat with shame and injury, even though they were far greater in number than those inside. And it is most miraculous <note>original: *het is op ’t hooghste te verwonderen*; literally "it is to be wondered at to the highest degree."</note> that in this battle, which lasted the entire day, only two men on the side of the **Evangelicals** <note>original: *Euangelischen*; the Protestant Waldensian believers.</note> perished, and two others were wounded, who recovered in a short time.

This great victory made the believers in the Valleys <note>The Waldensian Valleys of the Piedmont region.</note> courageous and caused great joy among them. Their enemies, on the other hand, were silenced and blushed with shame.

W A L D E N S I A N S. 115

And some who were with the Army spoke out openly, saying: "God fights for those people, and we are doing them violence and injustice." And this same thing was said throughout the whole of Piedmont.

The following day, one of the Captains <term>Captains (Hooft-luyden): High-ranking military officers.</term> handed over his remaining soldiers to the Lord de la Trinité, saying that he no longer wished to be bound to such actions, and so he departed from there.

Another Captain said that he had been in many wars, battle formations, and field battles, but that he had never seen soldiers so disheartened <note>original: *versaeght*; discouraged or filled with fear.</note> as these were whenever they were to fight against these people.

Others said that they had never been so completely disheartened in any war as they were now, and they attributed this to the earnest prayers of the Ministers <note>original: *Predikanten*; the Waldensian pastors.</note>.

After this, the Lord de la Trinité behaved as if he intended to take no further action against the Believers <note>The Waldensian Protestants.</note> from then on, but to leave them in peace; and to this end, he summoned four of the Reformed <term>Reformed (Gereformeerde): Those following the Protestant Reformation traditions.</term> to appear before him, with whom he spoke so persuasively that they believed his words and, upon their return, convinced many others as well. But how deceitful his words were, the outcome soon revealed.

For he very quickly dispatched a portion of his Army by night to Pre de la Cour <note>The Dutch "Pre de la Cour" refers to Pra del Tor, the natural mountain fortress and spiritual center of the Waldensians.</note>, where he had previously received so many blows; and the other part of his men

# WALDENSIANS. 117
