Even though the Count de la Trinité <note>The Count de la Trinité was the commander of the Duke of Savoy's forces tasked with suppressing the Waldensian "heresy" in the Piedmontese valleys.</note> withdrew, he nevertheless did not cease to threaten the Believers <note>original: *Geloovige*; used here to refer specifically to the Waldensian Protestants.</note>. But just as his threats reached their highest and most severe point, he fell into a great sickness.

On this occasion, the Lord of Raconis <note>A mediator who often stood between the Waldensian subjects and the Ducal court of Savoy.</note> attempted to establish a peace, for which the Papists <note>A historical term used by Protestants to refer to Roman Catholics.</note> had as great a longing as the Reformed: and for this reason, the Lord of Raconis was asked to appeal to the Duchess to persist in seeking a good peace.

After a few days, he gave them his answer: that the Duchess had prevailed upon the Duke to such an extent that they would be left in good peace and relieved of the presence of the soldiers. They would also obtain a full pardon for everything that had occurred in this war, and their consciences would not be burdened—under the condition that they would dismiss their Ministers <term>Ministers (Predikanten): The Protestant clergy or pastors. The Duke’s demand to "dismiss" them was a move to strip the community of its spiritual leadership.</term>, hold no public sermons, and accept the Mass <note>The central act of Roman Catholic worship, which the Waldensians had rejected in favor of their own Reformed services.</note> in their regions. As for the prisoners, they were also to be released, provided they paid their ransom.

But because the Reformed could not agree to such conditions, they sent...

<meta>This page continues the account of the peace negotiations mediated by the Lord of Raconis following the military stalemate in the Waldensian Valleys. It transitions from the Duchess's initial intervention to the formal drafting of a peace agreement.</meta>

Chronicle of the

Lord *de Raconis*, with a humble petition to the Duchess, in which the reasons why they could not accept such an agreement were recounted in detail. Whereupon the Lord *of Raconis* requested of the Reformed, through a letter, that they should send some of their own to him, so that through this the entire business might be concluded more speedily.

After not only once, but several times—indeed, for an entire month—deputies were sent to the Lord *of Raconis*, until finally, on the 5th day of *June*, in this year 1561, the following Accord <term>Accord (Accoordt): A formal peace treaty, specifically the Peace of Cavour, which ended the military conflict between the Duke of Savoy and his Protestant subjects.</term> was made, and sealed by the Lord *Raconis*, the Duke's nearest blood relative, as well as by the Ministers and other deputies of the Valleys <note>The "Valleys" refers to the specific Alpine regions—the Val Pellice, Val Chisone, and Val Germanasca—where the Waldensian communities were concentrated.</note>. And because it is somewhat lengthy, we shall only set it down here in brief. Namely:

That all those who had taken up arms in this war should have forgiveness for it.

That everyone was free to hold public preaching and other exercises of their religion, namely in those places where they were accustomed to hold and practice them openly; such as in the *Valley of Lucerna* <note>original: *Dal Lucerne*; the center of the Waldensian community.</note>, *Perosa* <note>original: *Perouse*.</note>, *San Martino* <note>original: *St. Martijn*.</note>; as also others which were likewise specified and expressed. In other places, everyone would be permitted to live, to attend the preachings, to call Ministers to their houses, and to use [their spiritual services] in times of need.

--- Page 137 ---
<meta>This page continues the specific terms of the Peace of Cavour (June 1561), a landmark treaty that granted the Waldensians limited religious tolerance. It follows the list of geographical areas where preaching was permitted.</meta>

### WALDENSIANS. 119

That all who had fled should return again, to possess and inhabit their houses and property, which should be returned and restored to them; namely those which had been taken from them on account of Religion or otherwise in the preceding wars.

That they should be left, sheltered, and protected in all their Liberties and Rights <term>Rights (Gerechtigheden): Specifically, the legal privileges and chartered freedoms granted to the Alpine communities by previous rulers.</term> obtained from His Highness and his illustrious ancestors.

That, where the Mass is held and the Roman Religion practiced in the Valleys, none of them should be required to attend such worship, nor to help promote it.

These and other similar Articles were promised and pledged to the Believers, after they had been most severely persecuted for eight months and attacked with war for seven months.

This peace negotiation greatly displeased the Pope; but the Duke <note>Duke Emmanuel Philibert of Savoy (1528–1580). While initially intent on enforcing Catholic uniformity, he agreed to the peace to stabilize his borders and economy.</note> informed him that such a peace was very necessary for all his subjects.

Yet the Roman Clergy, or the priesthood, could not rest; for the monks and other Orders arrived puffed up with their disputations <note>original: *disputatien*; formal theological debates intended to prove the Waldensian "heresy" wrong.</note>, through which, however, they always made their great ignorance manifest.

Whereby the Inquisitor, fearing that many more might be drawn away from the Roman Church through such disputations,

This prohibition was publicly proclaimed on the 6th day of May, in the year 1563.

This command did not please the Noblemen and others: they complained about this to the Duke <note>Duke Emmanuel Philibert of Savoy.</note>, and said that they necessarily had to deal with the Reformed; hereupon the Duke declared himself thus: that, namely, the fellowship between their two Religions, in worldly matters <note>original: *wereltlycke saken*; referring to trade, legal, and social interactions outside of worship.</note>, was not taken away by such a prohibition, but one should only abstain from speaking of Religion and of Faith.

In the year 1564, there came a Dominican Monk who desired to put the above-mentioned prohibition of the Inquisitor into effect; he went into the Reformed houses at Bibiana, Campiglione, and Fenile <note>Three villages in the Piedmont plain, on the border of the Waldensian valleys.</note> and wished to search their books; but it was shown to him that these places were also included in the Peace negotiations, and with this answer he was turned away.

For this reason, the Duke <note>Duke Emmanuel Philibert of Savoy.</note> also would not uphold the articles that he had granted them through the Lord of Raconis.

To this, their answer was that, even if one person or another might have acted against the established peace, it was nonetheless not just that the entire people should be punished for it. Therefore, they sent several representatives to the Duke to present their humble requests to him.

When Castrocaro <note>Sebastiano Grazioli of Castrocaro, the newly appointed and notoriously strict Governor of the Valleys.</note> noticed this, he also hurried to the Duke and soon after returned to the Lucerna Valley. When he had summoned the leaders of the congregations before him, he showed them that, namely, the Duke wished to forgive them for everything past and would uphold everything that his nephew, the Lord of Raconis, had promised them in his name—but on the condition that they would promise to keep what he would present to them in writing.

But he required that they should promise not to accept any foreign preacher who was not a native of the land; that the preachers should not write letters to other places; and that the Syndics <term>Syndics (Cindici): local magistrates or community representatives.</term>, along with other officials in the Valleys, should send no letters or books concerning religion to other places where the Duke had authority; that they should desire a Governor and make no alliance with other Lords; that one part of the community should not assist the other with armed force; and that they should accept the preachers that His Highness <note>original: *sijn Doorl.* (Doorluchtigheid); a title of respect for the Duke of Savoy.</note> would give them.

### Chronicle of the

Those of the Reformed who had been isolated—after they had been overcome with many crafty and deceptive words—signed all of these things.

But when the other leaders of the people <note>These were the "Hoofden," the secular and religious elders of the Waldensian communities.</note> heard of this, they were highly displeased; they placed under the Ban <term>Ban: a form of religious excommunication or social exclusion used to discipline members who compromised the community's safety or faith.</term> those who had consented to such a contract, and protested against their actions.

But all their protesting was in vain; in particular, *Castrocaro*, their greatest enemy, was forced upon them as Governor. And how he behaved toward them can easily be inferred from a few examples of the tyranny he committed.

When he learned that the Countess of *Cardé* and other nobles from lower *Piedmont* had come to *San Giovanni* <note>St. Joan (San Giovanni) was a key town in the Pellice Valley where Waldensian worship was often contested.</note> to receive the Holy Supper <note>The "Holy Supper" refers to the Protestant sacrament of Communion, which was a central point of theological conflict with the Catholic authorities.</note>, he arrived with his soldiers to prevent it.

He commanded the congregation at *Bobbio* <note>The village of Bobbio Pellice, located deep in the alpine valley.</note> to dismiss their preacher because he was a foreigner; and as they were unwilling to do so, he levied a fine against all the Reformed people in *Bobbio*.

Because he bore an exceedingly great hatred toward Lord *Gilles de Gilles* <note>Gilles de Gilles was a prominent Waldensian pastor and leader known for his steadfast resistance to Savoyard encroachment.</note>—as he was the one who had manfully opposed his aforementioned *Articles*—and being unable to drive him out under the pretext that he was a foreigner, he devised other means to dispose of him.

# THE WALDENSIANS.

F 2

He <note>Referring to Governor Castrocaro.</note> complained about him to the Duke, stating that he <note>The preacher Gilles de Gilles.</note> had been to Grenoble, to Geneva, and several other places to form an alliance between the Valleys <note>The Piedmontese alpine valleys where the Waldensian communities were situated.</note> and other Estates: upon this, it was ordered that he should be held prisoner. Castrocaro did this in great haste; he had him put in irons and, like a murderer, led to Turin: there they sought to force him to confess that he had been involved in such matters as had been alleged.

But when they could not obtain such a confession from him, Joseph Barberi—a great enemy of the Reformed <note>The "Reformed" refers to the Protestant movement, of which the Waldensians had become a part.</note>—attempted to move him to the Roman Religion <note>The Roman Catholic Church.</note> through many flattering words and promises: and he gave him a Book, in which he, as a sign of his conversion, was to write that he consented to it and believed everything that was written therein.

To this he gave as an answer: he could not do so unless he first knew the contents. Barberi left that book with him for three days, and after the passage of the three days, he demanded an answer from him. Then the Preacher said that the Book was full of errors and blasphemies; he would rather die than approve of such errors and change his religion. Barberi grew very angry at this answer and said: "Bring wood here, lay him upon it, and burn him, without further Trial <term>Trial (Proces): the formal legal proceedings or judicial inquiry which were often bypassed in cases of perceived heresy or political urgency.</term>." To this the Preacher answered: "I am in the hand of God and the judges, and I hope that no violence shall be done to me: and even if one wished to kill me against the law, I submit <note>original: "soo onderwerp"</note>..."

<header>Chronicle of the</header>

[...] myself to the will of **God**. Matters remained thus for that time.

But afterward, he <note>The preacher Gilles de Gilles.</note> was released through the intercession of the Duchess <note>Margaret of France, Duchess of Savoy, who was known for her sympathetic stance toward the Reformed faith.</note> and certain envoys or ambassadors from Germany.

In this year 1565, on the 20th of June, the Duke issued a Mandate <note>A formal decree or law.</note> stating that all those in his land who did not wish to live according to the Roman Religion <note>Roman Catholicism.</note> should declare themselves within ten days and depart from his land within two months.

And since this command was a general one, it was also sent to various congregations, which then caused unspeakable misery and a great multitude of *exulanten* <note>original: "exulanten"</note>, or exiles, of all social ranks and conditions.

Since this persecution was now a general and very severe one, the Believers, alongside earnest prayers to **God**, also took all other good means at hand: they requested the Duchess for her usual intercession, which she, for her part, did not fail to provide in everything that might have served them for the better. But because she could not prevent or turn away all misfortune as she desired, the Believers applied to certain German Princes—and especially to the Elector at Heidelberg and the Elector of Saxony—to seek intercession with his **Highness** <term>Highness (Doorl.): an abbreviation of "Doorluchtigheydt" (Serene Highness), the title of the Duke of Savoy.</term>; who immediately, in the year 1566, sent Lord *Johannes Junius*, a former Councilor of the Electoral Palatinate, to *Turin*.

The Envoy had not been in Turin long before he saw how proceedings were being taken against the Evangelicals <note>Protestants, specifically those adhering to the Reformed or "Gospel" faith.</note>. For the enemy of the Christians, namely Barberi <note>Likely a local official or inquisitor notorious for his hostility toward the Reformed faith.</note>, had the Envoy’s Secretary—after learning that he was a Preacher—removed from the Envoy’s lodging and cast into prison, despite the law of nations <note>The early modern concept of diplomatic immunity which should have protected the envoy's staff.</note> being invoked on his behalf.

The Envoy complained of this to his Serene Highness <term>Serene Highness: the Duke of Savoy, Emmanuel Philibert.</term>; whereupon the Secretary was released without delay, and Barberi was thrown into prison in his place.

The Councilor from the Electoral Palatinate <note>A representative of the Elector of the Palatinate, a powerful German prince who championed the Protestant cause.</note> made known to the Duke how his gracious Princes and Lords, moved by great love for their fellow believers, were compelled to petition him on their behalf: that he might leave them unmolested in their consciences, etc.

The Duke soon made it known through his answer that such intercession did not please him, as subsequent events would soon show. For he replied that he did not investigate how the Count Palatine of the Rhine, the Duke of Saxony, and other German Princes governed their subjects; and since he was the Sovereign Lord and recognized no other superiors, he was obliged to give an account of his actions to no one. Nevertheless, he thanked the aforementioned Electors and all others who meant well for him, his State, and his subjects, and who desired to bring them to a state of peace.

### Chronicle of the

...not to tolerate more than one religion in his land and territory; namely, that which he had found there and in which he had been raised. He did this so that he would not have to dispute and quarrel with his subjects under the pretext of religion and freedom of conscience, as he is, by rightful title, their Overlord and Sovereign of the Land—just as has happened to many princes in Europe who thereafter could no longer remain master over their subjects.

He had sufficiently made it known to his subjects that he was inclined toward peace: for that reason, he had kept the Waldensians <note>A medieval Christian movement that sought a return to Apostolic poverty and simplicity, later aligning with the Protestant Reformation.</note> in the Valleys of his territory under an Edict <note>The Treaty of Cavour (1561), which granted limited religious freedom to the Waldensians within specific Alpine valleys.</note>, which they were to observe inviolably. And whenever, outside of those Valleys, there were some stubborn heads who had a desire for novelty <note>Referring to religious innovations or the introduction of Reformed doctrines outside of the permitted areas.</note>, he had them punished as rebels. He believed that, just as the aforementioned Princes [of Germany] have compassion for their own subjects and help them toward the free exercise of their religion, they, as wise Princes, should not take it ill of him that he punishes the rebellious for the sake of peace and the maintenance of his State.

Yet, in consideration of their intercession <note>The formal request made by the German envoys on behalf of the persecuted Protestants.</note>, he wished to give better regard to those of his subjects who were their fellow believers and allow them some relief.

And while the Envoy made a special plea for Master Gille de Gilles <note>A prominent Waldensian pastor who had been imprisoned.</note>, of whom we made mention above, he was released from a very foul prison and placed in a chamber; and thereafter, since nothing else against him...

Likewise, the Duke also dismissed the Castellan of the Valley of Meana <note>A local administrative official.</note> because he had dealt too severely with the Waldensians.

On the other hand, the Envoy of the Elector-Palatine <note>The Elector-Palatine was one of the most powerful Protestant princes of the Holy Roman Empire, acting as a protector of the Reformed faith.</note> was not yet halfway on his journey home before the persecution began much more fiercely than before.

For Castrocaro <note>Andrea di Castrocaro, the governor of the valleys, who became notorious for his cruelty toward the Waldensian population.</note> immediately issued an Edict on April 21st of the year 1566, in which he commanded all those who lived under his rule and were not natives of the land—upon pain of life and limb and the loss of all their goods—to depart on the first day after this proclamation.

He also issued another Edict in which he forbade the Reformed in Luserna, Bibiana, Campiglione, and Fenile from attending the sermons at San Giovanni <note>San Giovanni was a key Waldensian center; these towns were often the site of disputes over where Protestant worship was legally permitted.</note>, under the same penalty.

But because these people, according to the treaty established in the year 1561 <note>The Peace of Cavour, which granted the Waldensians limited religious freedom in specific Alpine valleys.</note>, nevertheless attended the sermons at the aforementioned place, he seized them on the road, threw both men and women into prison, and had them punished according to his Edict.

But when the Reformed complained to the Duke regarding these unlawful proceedings, he was obliged to leave them unmolested.

# CHRONICLE OF THE

...territory that is called the *New Lands* <note>Known as the *Terres Neuves de Provence*, these territories in the County of Nice were under the control of the House of Savoy.</note>, at the end of the winter month <note>Likely February or March.</note>, it was proclaimed by trumpeters that whoever did not attend the Mass within the space of one month would, after that time, have to vacate the land upon pain of loss of life and property.

These poor people could find no way to escape where they would not be in extreme poverty or in danger of life and limb. For all those who were brought before the Parliament at *Aix* and were called *Lutherans* <note>original: "Lutrianen." This was a general derogatory term used by the authorities for any Protestant reformers, regardless of their specific denomination.</note> were burned at the stake.

Count *Honorat de Tendes* <note>Honorat II de Savoie, Count of Tende, who served as a powerful military commander and Governor in the region.</note> delivered everyone he captured into the hands of the executioner.

*Gonzaga*, Duke of *Nevers*, the King of *France's* Governor in the *Marquisate of Saluzzo* <note>An Alpine territory that was a flashpoint of religious and political conflict between France and the Duchy of Savoy.</note>, likewise had everyone he could catch executed and strangled in the year 1567.

Those who fell into the hands of the Archbishop of *Embrun* in *Dauphiné* <note>A province in southeastern France known for its Waldensian populations in the high valleys.</note> were forced to languish in desolate and stinking pits, or die of cold and hunger in the Tower.

So too were the believers in *Piedmont* hunted and banished, and there was no other way to flee but over a high and impassable mountain in the heart of winter, full of ice and snow, over which they might have fled during the night if it had been possible. Because they had with them not only a great number of young and helpless children, but also old and elderly men and women, such a flight was all the more difficult for them.

When they now wished to climb over this mountain around Christmas / many women and children froze to death from the cold; and those who arrived there / stayed in the Val Fressinière <note>original: "Dal Fraissiniere." In the year 1581, a presumptuous and arrogant Jesuit <note>A member of the Society of Jesus, a Catholic religious order known for its scholarly defense of the papacy and its leading role in the Counter-Reformation.</note> preached in the Luserna Valley <note>original: "Dal Lucerne." The Jesuit gave no written answer to this letter, but said only to the messenger who brought him the letter that he wished to dispute with the Minister orally and not in writing: and that he should tell him to prepare himself on the coming Sunday to debate with him in San Giovanni concerning the true Church, its true Head, and the perfection of Holy Scripture. And the Minister was satisfied with this.

When the Minister <note>A Protestant pastor, specifically from the Waldensian community.</note> was informed of this, he also took two other Ministers with him.

When they, along with many other people, appeared at the appointed place on the designated Sunday, the Jesuit did not come; instead, he traveled with a large entourage—which included the Lieutenant of Governor Castrocaro <note>Pantaleon de Castrocaro, the governor of the Piedmont valleys, known for his fluctuating and often harsh treatment of the Waldensians.</note>—to Villar <note>Villar Pellice, a village further up the valley from San Giovanni.</note>, where he expected to find the people without a Minister and intended to preach there according to his own whim. In the meantime, the Minister arrived at Villar and reproached the Jesuit for his flight.

Shortly thereafter, those who had waited for him at San Giovanni <note>original: "S. Jan"</note> also arrived, demanding that he should debate at Villar just as he had promised to do at San Giovanni. But he had no desire for it and said: he had not been sent to debate with the Minister, but to instruct the people.

There he was sternly reproached for the great boldness and impudence with which he had requested such a debate, and for inviting such a large and distinguished company to hear it. But no, the "spirit of debate" was no longer to be found in him.

### WALDENSIANS.

...had <note>original: "hadde"</note> on both sides <note>Presumably meaning both the Protestant and Catholic sides had reasons or opportunities to stop the conflict from escalating, or that the Jesuit found excuses on both sides.</note> to prevent this disputation. With this, this fearful Father <note>The Dutch uses "Paes," which literally means Pope, but was often used as a derogatory or general term for a Catholic priest; here it refers to the Jesuit mentioned previously.</note> went away with his company; nor did he say that he was inclined to debate in the future.

Nevertheless, eight months later, on a Sunday, he came with a large following to St. Jean <note>The village of San Giovanni Pellice, a Waldensian stronghold.</note>, greeted the Minister, and made it known that he was the one who previously wanted to debate him; but because the opportunity at that time would not allow it, he now wanted to learn from him whether he recognized the Baptism of the Papists <note>A term used by reformers for Roman Catholics.</note> as valid or not. Likewise, whether he considered those of the Roman Church to be believers or unbelievers.

When the Minister noticed what he intended with these questions, he answered the question with such distinction and so clearly that, even though the Jesuit thought he had not yet been given enough, the most prominent men on his own side admitted it and said: "Father, he has answered you enough, let us go."

After eight days he came again to the same place with new questions, to which he was answered again as the situation required.

And when he came there again after the lapse of eight days, a proper disputation <term>disputation|A formal, public academic or theological debate, usually conducted according to strict logical rules.</term> was organized in the presence of Governor Castrocaro <note>Pantaleon de Castrocaro, the governor of the Piedmont valleys.</note> and a great multitude of people of both religions.

The Jesuit always wanted to run from one question to the other and not stay on one matter, until finally the Governor brought it to the point that one should, at once, [speak] of one matter...

But when they [the Jesuits] received these rebuttals / they lost all courage to debate: they fled from the Valleys / and left the Reformed <note>The Waldensians, who had aligned themselves with the Protestant Reformation</note> unmolested / until the time that they learned that many Catholic <note>original: "Papiste"; a contemporary term for Roman Catholics, often used by Protestants</note> Princes had allied themselves / to call for the eradication of all the Reformed / and not to lay down their arms / until they had accomplished this.

# CHRONICLE OF THE

For news arrived daily of how the Reformed <note>The Waldensian Protestants</note> in Dauphiné <note>original: "Delphinat"; a province in southeastern France bordering the Waldensian valleys</note> had taken up arms / and had captured various fortified places: and that the same had occurred in the other provinces of France. Thus, the Duke <note>The Duke of Savoy</note> also took no action at this time against his Reformed subjects / and they remained for several years in a tolerable condition.

In the year 1590, in the absence of the Duke / the inhabitants of Meane and Maties <note>Modern Meana di Susa and Mattie in the Piedmont region</note>, located near Susa / were attacked on account of their religion: but after they fled to the Duchess for refuge / and showed her the freedoms obtained from Duke Emanuel Philibert <note>Emanuel Philibert (1528–1580), who had signed the Treaty of Cavour in 1561 granting limited rights to the Waldensians</note> / these same freedoms were granted to them / and they were left in peace / after they had paid six hundred and fifty crowns.

At this time, the King of France had an army in Piedmont for two and a half years / which captured many places: yet as long as this war lasted / the Reformed were not interrogated on account of their religion.

In the year 1596, the Jesuits were again earnest in their disputes; and one among them boasted that he would prove / that the present-day Roman Church is the true Church / and that outside of the same no salvation is to be obtained.

In this year, the Monks persistently urged the Governor of Ponte until he forbade those in Pinasca <note>original: "Pinasche"; a village in the Val Perosa</note>, in the Perosa Valley, from performing religious services in the customary church; ordering a guard to prevent such activities.

The Reformed <note>The Waldensian Protestants</note> demonstrated to the Governor that this church belonged to them by virtue of a public treaty they had made with the Papists <note>original: "Papisten"; a period term for Roman Catholics</note>; but he paid no heed to this.

When the Reformed had gathered together the following Sunday to hear the preaching, they opened the church and listened to the sermon: this angered the Governor; he decided therefore, with the help of others, to ambush the Reformed and their Minister on the following Sunday.

The Minister of Pinasca was also scheduled to preach at Villar <note>Villar Perosa</note> at that same time. When all the people were gathered in the church, the enemy arrived there unnoticed; however, a shepherd in the field spotted them and cried out with a loud voice. The people ran out of the church and covered him <note>the Minister</note> with a peasant's hat and cloak, so that he escaped from there in that manner.

Since they did not find the Minister, whom they were primarily seeking, they marched toward Pinasca, plundering, causing damage, and capturing whatever they could; after that, they also robbed the Minister of everything they could find: and be-

In the year 1598, a dispute was held in the Church at **St. Germain** <note>San Germano Chisone, a Waldensian village in the Perosa Valley</note> between a Minister and a Capuchin Monk <note>A member of an austere branch of the Franciscan order, often active in the Counter-Reformation</note>, and this with the consent of the Duke and the Papal Nuncio <note>The Pope’s diplomatic representative</note>, and the Inquisition at **Turin**.

The Monk wished to maintain that the essential body of Christ, after the Consecration <note>The ritual act of blessing the bread and wine during the Mass</note>, was present under the form of Bread and Wine. The dispute lasted several hours; but because the Monk's company (for many Clerical and Secular persons had come with him) had no desire to stay there any longer, they broke it off.

Afterward, the Minister alone had the accounts printed, which the appointed scribes <note>original: "Schrijvers"; people hired to take shorthand notes of the debate</note> had recorded. But because the Monk received little praise from his own side for this, the Inquisition strictly forbade all Papists <note>A contemporary Protestant term for Roman Catholics</note> from either buying or reading that book; furthermore, the Monk had learned so much from the Minister that neither he nor his fellow monks had any desire for further disputation.

# Chronicle of the

Since from time immemorial our dear ancestors, from father to son, have been instructed in the **Doctrine** and **Religion** which we have known from our childhood, and in which we have instructed our own, in the same manner as we were taught by our elders.

Also, while the **King of France** <note>original: *Vranckrijck*</note> held the **Marquisate of Saluzzo** <note>original: *Marck-graeffschap Saluces*</note>, we were permitted to profess and practice our **Religion** without hindrance or persecution; just as our brothers in the **Valleys of Lucerne**, **Perosa** <note>original: *Peruse*</note>, and others, who—following a special agreement established by our **Sovereign** <term>Landts-Vorst|Refers to the Duke of Savoy, the territorial ruler</term>—have enjoyed the free practice of the **Reformed Religion** <note>The Protestant faith adopted by the Waldensians</note> until this very day.

Nevertheless, his **Highness** <note>The Duke of Savoy</note>, incited more by evil counsel and by passionate people who begrudge us than by his own pleasure and will, intended to burden us, and to 그 end allowed an **Edict** to be issued against us.

By this, it should become known and public to everyone that we are being thus plagued and deprived of our property not for any crime we might have committed against the person of our **Prince**, nor because we have acted in rebellion against his **Edict**.

Acknowledge and make known to everyone that we know and assure ourselves that this **Doctrine and Religion**, which is taught and practiced there in the **Reformed Churches**, as well as in **France**...

->## WALDENSIANS.<-

-ce, <note>Completes the word "France" from the previous page</note> as in the Swiss Confederacy <note>original: *Eedt-genootſchap*</note>, Germany, Geneva, England, Scotland, Denmark, Sweden, Poland and other Kingdoms, Lands, and Lordships—which we have until now openly confessed—is the only true Christian Religion under the obedience of our Sovereigns <term>Landts-Vorſten|The Dukes of Savoy or other territorial princes</term> and high Authorities. It is ordained and confirmed by God, and it alone can make us acceptable to God and lead us to Salvation. So have we also resolved, as long as we live, to adhere to and follow the same, even if we should stake and lose our lives, honor, and property for it.

And if anyone should claim that we are mired in error, we request of him that he show us this same error; and we offer ourselves to immediately abjure it and to follow him who can show us a better way. We desire nothing more than that we may follow the true Religion with a quiet Conscience, which we poor creatures owe to our Creator, and that through such means we may come to true and eternal Salvation. But if one wishes to force us by violence alone to leave the right path of Salvation and to follow error, superstition, and the teachings of false men, then we would rather leave behind our houses and goods, yes, our own lives.

Herewith we most humbly pray His Highness <note>original: *Doorlucht[igheydt]*</note>, whom we [hold] as our na- <note>The text cuts off here at the catchword "tuur-"</note>

### Chronicle of the
Page 150

-tural Prince and Lord, [that] he would not allow us to be attacked without cause; but would ordain that we may spend the remaining time of our lives, with our children and descendants, in the obedience and service that we have at all times shown to them as upright and faithful Subjects <term>Onderdanen|Loyal residents of a territory under a sovereign</term>: as we desire nothing else from her Highness <note>original: *Doorlucht.*, a formal honorific title for a ruler</note> than that we give to them that which we are bound to by the express command of God. That we may also give to God the Lord that honor and service which we owe him, and which he demands of us in his Holy Word.

We also request, in the midst of our misery and oppression <note>original: *drucks*, meaning pressure, distress, or affliction</note>, from the Reformed Churches, that they will recognize us as true members thereof; we wish with our blood, if it should so please God, to subscribe to their Confession of Faith <term>Geloofs-bekentenisse|A formal statement of religious belief common in Protestant traditions</term>, which we find in all parts, through and through, to be conformable to the Doctrine which the Holy Apostles taught and wrote, and for that reason to be true and Apostolic.

We also promise to live and die in the same: and when we are seized and persecuted for this, we then thank God that he shows us the honor that we may suffer for his Name's sake; and we commend the outcome of our actions, and the justice of our cause, into the hands of his Divine providence, which shall deliver us when and by what means it shall please him.

--- Page 169 ---
<meta>This page concludes the petition of the Waldensian exiles from Saluzzo (begun on the previous page) and transitions into a new account of persecution in the Valley of Lucerna in 1602.</meta>

### W A L D E N S I A N S.

We humbly pray to that same faithful God of ours, that, just as he holds the hearts of all Kings and Princes in his hand, he may also move the heart of his Highness <note>original: *Doorlucht.*, a formal honorific title for a sovereign ruler</note> to a will of mercy toward those who never offended him, nor ever thought to do so; so that he may find them to be much more loyal and upright in his service than those who incite and drive him to such persecution.

If, on the contrary, it pleases God to leave us in such persecution, then we pray that he would be pleased to arm us with patience and steadfastness, to persevere in the knowledge of the truth until our end, and our descendants after us, Amen.

Following this terrible persecution in the Marquisate <term>March-graefschap|A territory governed by a marquess</term> of Saluzzo, another began in the following year of 1602 in the Valley of Lucerna <note>Now the Val Pellice in the Piedmont region of Italy</note>. For at that time there arrived in Lucerna the Count of Lucerna, Governor Ponte, and Archbishop Broglia, with a large group of monks and Jesuits. All of these took up residence in the Count's palace and summoned all the heads of households <note>original: *Huys-vaders*, literally "house-fathers," representing the family unit</note> who belonged to the Reformed Religion before them. They declared to them that the Duke was not minded to tolerate anyone of the Reformed Religion in the Valley of Lucerna anymore. They were therefore to make a decision: either to attend Mass <term>Misse|The central liturgical service of the Roman Catholic Church</term>, or to abandon everything and depart from the country, never to return. Some suffered a sorrowful fall <note>meaning they converted to Catholicism under pressure</note>; but the majority remained steadfast.

<meta>This page continues the account of the 1602 persecution of the Waldensians in the Piedmontese valleys. # Chronicle of the

From Lucerna, these Lords traveled to Bibiana <note>original: *Bubiane*, a municipality in the Metropolitan City of Turin</note>, where the majority and most prominent of the people also belonged to the Reformed Religion. There, they disclosed the same command; but not a single person was willing to attend Mass <term>Misse|The central liturgical service of the Roman Catholic Church</term>, for they had all been very earnestly exhorted to steadfastness by several of their leaders.

Consequently, four of the most prominent men were summoned to Turin in the hope that they would show such high honor to the Duke <note>Charles Emmanuel I, Duke of Savoy (reigned 1580–1630)</note> that they would agree to attend Mass.

When they arrived there, the first was brought alone before the Duke, who immediately requested that he attend Mass. But he replied that he could not do so without violating his conscience. The Duke said that he desired to be saved just as much as the Reformed; and if he did not consider his own religion—namely, the Roman Catholic Religion—to be the true one, he would not urge anyone else to embrace it. After this, he dismissed the man, who left feeling joyful and comforted; however, he did not dare to reveal what had happened to the others who had been summoned with him.

When the others were then brought before the Duke, they were told that their companion had submitted to the Duke’s will and had promised to change his religion; and that if they did not wish to do the same, they could expect nothing but trouble <note>original: *quaedts*, meaning harm, evil, or misfortune</note>.

<meta>catchword: Als</meta>

<summary>Authorities move their efforts to convert the Reformed population from Lucerna to Bibiana and summon four leaders to Turin, where the Duke attempts to use deception to force their conversion.</summary>
<keywords>Bibiana, Turin, Duke of Savoy, Reformed Religion, Mass, religious interrogation, steadfastness, conscience</keywords>

--- Page 171 ---
<meta>This page continues the account of the four Waldensian delegates summoned to Turin. On the previous page, the first delegate had remained steadfast, but the authorities lied to the remaining three, telling them their companion had converted to Catholicism.</meta>

-> # W A L D E N S E N. <-

When they were now brought before the Duke, he addressed them in the same manner as he had the previous man. And they gave for their answer: that if they could be left to practice their own Religion, they would consider that a great favor. But if the Duke did not please to allow this, they were willing to submit themselves to his will. At this, the Duke was well satisfied and made them great promises. However, when they returned to their companion and heard from him how he had actually conducted himself and how things had truly gone for him, they fell into a great sorrow that they had allowed themselves to be so easily swayed. They confessed their error <note>original: *mis-slag*, a mistake or lapse in judgment</note> and, shortly thereafter, returned once again to the Reformed Church.

Meanwhile, the Archbishop and his monks did not cease their efforts to incite the Reformed in Bibiana <note>original: *Bubiane*</note> and other nearby places to apostasy <term>afval|The abandonment of one's religious faith, in this context, leaving Protestantism for Catholicism</term> through disputations, kind words, and generous promises.

But Governor Ponte undertook to bring the believers to apostasy through threats and harsh edicts <term>Placcaten|Official public decrees or ordinances</term>. Among other measures, he had it proclaimed that any inhabitants of Lucerna, Bibiana, Campiglione <note>original: *Campillon*</note>, and Fenile <note>original: *Fenil*</note> who refused to attend Mass must leave the country within five days, under penalty of death <note>original: *op lijfs-straffe*</note>.

To prevent this great evil, the people of the Lucerna Valley appointed certain representatives to go to the Duke, in order to humbly—

disputation was this: that the Jesuit wanted to prove that Christ had taught and ordained the Mass / and that the same was to be found in the Holy Scripture.

As they were now in the heat of the dispute / Master Gros demanded of the Jesuit / that he should prove all parts of the Mass / one after the other / from the Holy Scripture. Then the Jesuit was so hard-pressed / that he had to confess outright / that a large part of the Mass / or the Mass-service / had been added or joined to it by the Church / from time to time. Thereupon Master Gros said with a loud voice / since the Jesuit had to confess that those parts of the Mass / which the Reformed <term>Gereformeerden|Protestants following the Reformed tradition, here specifically the Waldensians</term> reject / were instituted neither by Christ nor by the Apostles; then he himself spoke against the practice of attending Mass / and ought also to hold all his listeners to that same view / that when one removes all these superstitious additions / only that which Christ ordained / and the Apostles used / should be kept. He said furthermore / how unreasonable and barbaric it is / to torture and persecute the true Christians so horribly / since they hold themselves only to the institution of Christ / and follow the practice of the early Apostolic Church.

Through this action / the absent Believers <note>Those Waldensians who could not attend the debate in person</note> were comforted and strengthened; but the opponents were saddened and put to shame.

And since the day was already at an end / the dispute came to an end: the Jesuit had not

-> W A L D E N S I A N S. <-

thereafter had no more courage to dispute openly.

When the Archbishop and his followers saw that they could accomplish nothing with open disputes; they brought about an Edict / through which all schools and public worship in many places / as well as all dealings between both religions / were forbidden to the Reformed <term>Gereformeerde|Followers of the Reformed or Calvinist tradition, specifically the Waldensians in this context</term>.

Around this time, grain in Piedmont was so expensive / that it could not be bought for any money; but everyone who was willing to attend Mass was promised grain for free / as much as they would need. They were also given money and other things / to persuade them more easily: which then achieved more with some poor people / than all their disputes.

But this practice was not even liked by the Papists <term>Papisten|A contemporary term used by Protestants to refer to Roman Catholics</term> at Bibiana <note>original: "Bubiane"</note>: for they saw that their numbers increased / not out of love for their religion / but because of the grain and money given to the poor.

From the Lucerna Valley <note>The Val Pellice, one of the three Waldensian valleys</note>, the Archbishop traveled to the Perosa Valley / and first attempted at Bobbio <note>original: "Bubion"</note> to move those people / to attend Mass. But since his words and promises were not so powerful / that they could move a single person: he traveled to Perosa; stayed there for a long time / and distributed money / grain / bread / and other things among those poor / who were willing to attend Mass. abandoned their religion / even though the scarcity was very great: and also those / who had gone to the **Mass** out of necessity and want / returned / after the **Bishop's** departure / once again to the **Reformed Church** <term>Gereformeerde Kercke|The Protestant church following the Calvinist tradition, which the Waldensians had joined during the Reformation</term>.

When they could also accomplish little through this means / the restless **Priests** and **Monks** petitioned the **Duke** / that the **Reformed** / who lived in the **Perosa Valley** <note>original: "Dal Peruse"</note> along the great **Highroad** / because they caused great offense to the passing **Papists** <term>Papisten|A term used by Protestants for Roman Catholics</term> / should have to move to other places.

This **Edict** was issued / and published; but it pleased the **Papists** as little / as the **Reformed**: for this reason both parties petitioned the **Duke** / that they might be left in their old dwellings; which then / after long persistence / occurred.

When it was learned / around this time / that / in the **Marquisate of Saluzzo** <note>original: "March-graeffschap van Saluces"</note> / after the others had been driven out / some **Reformed** were still staying there secretly / they were diligently sought out / and also expelled.

These everlasting persecutions caused there to be many **Bandits** <term>Bandyten|Refers to "Barbets" or displaced persons and resistance fighters who took to the mountains and conducted raids during the religious wars</term> / who / back and forth / did no small amount of harm: for whose sake the **Reformed** were in great fear and anxiety; and the **Papists** in particular suffered great nuisance from these same **Bandits**.

Now so that this great evil might be prevented / and that the **Reformed** again in

In the following year, 1603, the Duke <note>Charles Emmanuel I, Duke of Savoy</note> returned again from Nice to Turin / and appointed a judge / and afterward the Count of Lucerne / sending them into the Valleys / to investigate how the unrest had arisen in his absence / and who was principally to blame for it.

The Count of Lucerne behaved very kindly toward the Reformed / and exhorted both parties / of both Religions <note>Referring to the Catholic and the Reformed (Waldensian) inhabitants.</note> / with great earnestness / to peace and unity ; he also promised them (on the condition / that if the Valleys of Lucerna / Perosa, and St. Martin / would advance <note>original: "opschieten" - to pay or provide a sum of money</note> two thousand five hundred <term>Ducats|A gold coin used as currency throughout Europe; here, a significant tax or "benevolence" payment to the Duke.</term>) in the name of the Duke / upon their submitted Petition <note>original: "Supplicatie"</note> ; these following liberties:

That the Duke did not wish that they / on account of Religion / should be harassed / provided that they only practiced it in the permitted places of the Valleys of Lucerna / Perosa, and St. Martin / to which they could again come unhindered : he wished to show all mercy / except to five / who / on account of their misconduct / had deserved a serious punishment ; the others would not be investigated on account of religion / etc.

Upon this, these churches were in a tolerable state / until the year 1611 ; but at the beginning of this year the Count de la Roche came / from <term>Dauphiné|A historical province in southeastern France, bordering the Piedmont valleys.</term> <note>original: "Delphinat"</note> / with many people / in the service of the Duke / into the Valley of Lucerna / and caused the inhabitants there great distress. # Chronicle of the
-> W A L D E N S I A N S. 162 <-

...had committed; they were to be taken to their Chief Magistrate <term>Potestaet|From the Italian 'Podestà', a high-ranking official or governor acting on behalf of the Duke.</term> in Lucerna <note>original: "Lucerne"; the town of Luserna San Giovanni, a regional administrative center in the Waldensian valleys.</note>. This was indeed done. But as there was no one to accuse these defendants, they were released.

When the Duke <note>Duke Charles Emmanuel I of Savoy.</note>, in the years 1613 and 1614, waged war in Montferrat against the King of Spain, and the **Reformed** also distinguished themselves in that same war with great praise, they encountered people there who spoke of their Religion and said that nothing was more desirable to them than that they might live in the same faith.

But they also found others, especially those who lived in remote, lonely places, to whom strange and miraculous things had been told about the **Waldensians**. It had been said of their soldiers that a regiment would come from the Piedmont Valley, among whom only a few were Christians, while the rest were strange and monstrous people who spoke ill of God, the Virgin Mary, and the deceased Saints. Some of them reportedly had only one eye, right in the middle of the forehead; a fourfold set of teeth with long black fangs; and it was claimed that they roasted their children over the fire and ate them. With many more such strange things, they were accused and made to be hated.

In some places, people both young and old came running to see such strange wonders; in other places, they all fled away from such monsters and trembled before them.

# -> W A L D E N S I A N S. <-

Some of the **Reformed** <note>Meaning the Waldensians, who by this time had formally joined the Protestant Reformation.</note>, when they returned, recounted that upon arriving in a certain small village one evening, the inhabitants stood before them with fear and trembling; however, they comforted them and, as much as possible, sought to alleviate their fear.

In a **locksmith's** house, when the evening meal was prepared, the **Reformed** requested water to wash their hands. The host said: "What, are you then Christians? We were told that in your troop <note>original: "rotte"; a term often used for a band or gang, here suggesting a lack of divine order.</note> there were no Christians, and in the others only a few, and that one could recognize them by this: namely, that Christians wash their hands before they eat, but the others do not."

Then the **Reformed** made them understand that these were gross lies and false accusations. They also prayed before and after the meal, and conducted themselves in word and deed in such a manner that the inhabitants of that place were amazed. They spoke continually with these **Waldensians**, showing them great love and friendship.

At about this same time, those at **St. Joan** <note>San Giovanni Pellice, a key town in the Waldensian valleys.</note>, on account of the great number of people, had a church built, which they used for their worship without hindrance for a long time.

In the year 1618, the two brothers **Carolus** and **Emanuel**, Counts in the Valley of **Lucerne** <note>The Luserna family were the traditional feudal lords of the Waldensian valleys.</note>, passed away. Although they were **papists** <note>A historical term for Roman Catholics.</note>, they nevertheless always upheld the **Reformed** in their legal rights...

...they maintained <note>This continues from the previous page, referring to the Waldensians maintaining their favor or status.</note>, especially for this reason: because the **Elector of Saxony** <note>The ruler of Saxony, one of the most powerful Protestant princes in the Holy Roman Empire.</note> had grandly received the aforementioned **Carolus** in **Dresden** when he traveled to the **Emperor** in **Prague** in the name of the Duke. Following this, many prominent persons and others joined together and practiced their prayers in certain places, read the **Holy Scripture**, and observed other godly exercises as time permitted.

And God so blessed this matter that in a short time, in various places, populous gatherings were found, to which the nearest-residing **Ministers** <note>original: "Predikanten"</note> sometimes came to preach and to administer the **Sacraments**.

### WALDENSIANS.

...they also earnestly insisted that they might be protected in that same freedom in the future. But the power and influence of their opponents were so great that finally harsh <term>Edicts</term> <note>original: "Placcaten"; these were official public decrees or laws issued by a sovereign authority, often used to enforce religious conformity.</note> were issued against them, so that they were banished, thrown into prisons, killed, and persecuted in many other ways.

And although the issued Edicts only concerned those who had joined the <term>Reformed Religion</term> <note>The branch of Protestantism following the teachings of John Calvin, which the Waldensians had formally adopted in the 16th century.</note> in the <term>Marquisate of Saluzzo</term> <note>original: "Marck-graeffchap Saluces"; a territory in the Piedmont region of modern-day Italy.</note>, the enemies of the truth nevertheless sought to use them against those who lived in the Valleys of <term>Piedmont</term>. For this reason, they had the decrees published even in <term>Campiglione</term> <note>original: "Campillon"</note> and in the <term>Val Lucerna</term> <note>original: "Dal Lucerne"; one of the primary Alpine valleys inhabited by the Waldensians.</note>; however, this was not done openly in the usual manner, but in secret, after the holding of the <term>Mass</term> <note>By publishing decrees only after Catholic services, the authorities ensured that the Protestants—who did not attend Mass—would be unaware of the new laws until they had already broken them.</note>.

In these Edicts was, among other things, this article: that it would not be permitted for the Reformed to bury their dead in the churchyards of the <term>Papists</term> <note>original: "Papisten"; a common 17th-century Protestant term for Roman Catholics.</note>. Furthermore, they were not to allow more than six persons to be present at their burials, and those were to be without weapons.

Because the Reformed did not know of this Edict, they attempted to bury one of their dead according to their usual custom in their own churchyard, which was attached to the churchyard of the Papists but was distinguished from it by specific markers. At that location, a <term>Catholic Nobleman</term> <note>original: "Paeps Edelman"</note> with some of his followers opposed them and would not allow the deceased to be buried. Because of this, they brought the body back to the house, and among

### WALDENSIANS.

Reformed believers from the **Val Lucerna** <note>original: "Dal Lucerne"; one of the core Alpine valleys of the Waldensian community.</note> / who had come to the market there / were cast into prison: and these were held so long / until the 6,000 **Ducats** <note>A high-value gold coin; this sum represents a massive extortion of the local population.</note> from the inhabitants of the **Val Lucerna**, **Saint-Martin** <note>original: "St. Martijn"; the Val San Martino.</note> and **Perosa** <note>original: "Peruse"; the Val Perosa.</note> / were raised and paid / and thus the Duke confirmed their freedom once again.

In the beginning of the year 1622, there arrived in the Valleys a <term>Bull</term> <note>An official papal decree, named for the "bulla" or lead seal attached to it.</note> from **Pope Gregory XV** <note>original: "Gregorii des xv."</note> / in which the Pope granted the Duke of **Savoy** a <term>tithe</term> <note>original: "tienden"; a tenth part of ecclesiastical income or church property.</note> of the church properties in his lands for six years / provided that he would assist him in the extermination of the heretics <note>The term used by the Catholic Church for the Waldensians, who were considered dissidents.</note>.

The fruit of this grant immediately appeared or was revealed: for in that same year all the Reformed from **Praviglielmo** <note>original: "Pravillelm"; a remote mountain parish in the Val Lucerna.</note> / were summoned before the Governor at **Saluzzo** <note>original: "Saluces"; a city in the Piedmont region of modern-day Italy.</note> / under penalty of death / and upon the forfeiture of all their property.

Those summoned did not dare to appear: for they feared / that they might be detained / because they had not moved away / as others had done ten years prior; and also because they did not attend the **Mass** <note>original: "Misse"; the central Catholic ritual. Refusal to attend was often used as legal proof of heresy.</note>.

When they did not appear / the Governor banished them forever from the Duke's lands / confiscated their property / and threatened / wherever they could be caught / to hang them. This judgment was also confirmed by the Council at **Turin**.

Since they were now in such fear and distress / and could find no means to survive, [they] sought <note>This sentence continues onto the next page, following the catchword "sochten" (sought).</note>

they sought the Lord **Lesdiguières** <note>François de Bonne, Duke of Lesdiguières (1543–1626), a prominent French military leader who had recently converted from Protestantism to Catholicism to become Constable of France.</note>, even though he had already changed his religion / asking that he would advocate for them with the Duke / which he did with all seriousness / and achieved so much / that the passed judgment was not carried out.

In this year, a Captain / named **Frans Garnier of Dronero** <note>original: "Frans Garnier van Dronier"; Dronero is a town in the province of Cuneo.</note> / who had moved to the **Val Lucerna** <note>original: "Dal Lucerne"; one of the primary Waldensian valleys.</note> because of his religion / was captured by the Governor of that place as he traveled past **Dronero** / and kept in the Castle of Dronero. In all cities / towns and villages / while his godless escorts ate and drank in the inns / they left him / as a spectacle / bound with chains / fastened outside the door / standing.

When he was now brought into **Turin** / he was first thrown into the prison / called the **Purgatory** <note>original: "Vage-buur" (Vagevuur); a common name for a specific section of a prison intended to "purify" the prisoner through hardship.</note> / and afterwards into the one / called **Hell** / in which he / by his fellow prisoners / was mocked, slandered, and mistreated in the most hideous way.

# W A L D E N S I A N S. 169
### P
<meta>catchword: Anno</meta>

Meanwhile, his relatives <note>original: "bloedt-vrienden"; literally "blood-friends," meaning kinsmen or family relations.</note>, who were many in number, did not cease to petition for his release: whereupon the Duke ordered that his case should be processed without delay; and when it was found that he was innocent, he was released; but on the condition that he would return to **Dronero** <note>original: "Dronier"</note> and engage in further religious debates.

After his release, he returned to the **Val Lucerna**: and when he later wished to return home from a certain journey through **Provence** and **Nice**, he was met on the road by some Papists <note>A term used by Protestants at the time to refer to Roman Catholics.</note> of his acquaintance, who led him with them to **Piedmont** and, out of hatred for his religion, murdered him in a remote place.

In the following year, 1623, the persecution against those who still remained in the **Val de Barcelonnette** <note>original: "Dal Barcellone"; a valley in the French Alps that was then part of the Duchy of Savoy.</note> began violently. A decree was published by which all Reformed believers were commanded either to attend the Mass or to appear in **Turin** before the **Cardinal of Savoy** <note>Cardinal Maurice of Savoy (1593–1657), who was active in efforts to convert the Waldensians.</note>. Although they sought to prevent this persecution through frequent appeals, the aforementioned clergy, through their constant incitement, achieved so much that a new command was issued, by which all were ordered to hear the sermons of the monks under penalty of life and limb <note>original: "lijf- en levens-ſtraffe"; a legal term for capital punishment or severe corporal punishment.</note>: but as they would not do so, they were forced to leave everything and depart. Some then went to **Dauphiné** <note>original: "Delphinat"; a province in southeastern France.</note>, some to **Orange**, others to **Lyon**, and still others went into the valleys of **Piedmont**.

<summary>This page describes the release and subsequent murder of Captain Frans Garnier by his acquaintances, and details the 1623 decree forcing the Reformed inhabitants of the Val de Barcelonnette to choose between Catholicism, legal trial, or exile.</summary>
<keywords>Frans Garnier, Dronero, Val Lucerna, Val de Barcelonnette, Turin, Cardinal of Savoy, religious persecution, exile, Dauphiné, Lyon</keywords>

--- Page 188 ---
<meta>This page continues the historical account from 1623 into 1624, moving from the individual story of Captain Frans Garnier to the broader military operations against the Reformed communities in the Val Perosa and the resistance encountered by the Duke's forces.</meta>

# CHRONICLE OF THE... 170
<meta>catchword: Dies</meta>

In the year 1624, the Duke <note>Duke Charles Emmanuel I of Savoy (r. 1580–1630).</note> ordered many troops to be recruited: he sent them into **Val Perosa** <note>original: "Dal Peruse"; one of the three principal Waldensian valleys in the Piedmont region.</note>, and commanded the Reformed <note>The Waldensians or members of the Protestant Reformed Church.</note> to demolish six of their churches; and should they refuse to do so, then **Count Taffin** and **Savine**, a nobleman from **Dauphiné** <note>original: "Delphinat"; a province in southeastern France.</note>, would compel them by force with his regiment of Frenchmen. They pleaded earnestly to be left with their churches and freedoms, but it was all in vain.

However, because there were many soldiers among those French troops who were adherents of the Reformed religion, they did not wish to be used against their fellow believers; instead, they requested their discharge, which was granted to them. Nevertheless, some of them remained; when the Evangelicals <note>Protestant believers; in this context, the Waldensians.</note> were attacked, these soldiers joined them and stayed with them until the enemy had once again retreated.

When the aforementioned regiment reached the lower parts and the villages situated on the plains of **San Germano** <note>original: "St. Germain"; San Germano Chisone.</note>, the inhabitants, as they could offer no resistance, abandoned their houses and retreated to the heights, and from there they appealed for help from the neighboring valleys; but the passes were occupied by the enemy in such a manner that assistance could not easily reach them. Furthermore, those of **Val Lucerna** <note>original: "Dal Lucerne"; one of the primary Waldensian valleys.</note> were forbidden, under penalty of life and limb <note>original: "lijf- en levens-straffe"; a legal term for capital punishment or severe corporal punishment.</note>, to provide any assistance to the fugitives; however, even this could not entirely prevent them from doing so.

<summary>In 1624, the Duke of Savoy attempted to force the Reformed inhabitants of Val Perosa to destroy their own churches, leading to a military standoff where French Protestant soldiers refused to fight their own coreligionists.</summary>
<keywords>Val Perosa, Val Lucerna, San Germano Chisone, Dauphiné, Duke of Savoy, Count Taffin, Savine, Reformed Church, military resistance, religious solidarity</keywords>

--- Page 189 ---
<meta>This page continues the account of the 1624 military campaign against the Waldensian communities in the Piedmontese valleys, focusing on the diplomatic maneuvers of Count Taffin and the eventual betrayal of the truce in Val Perosa.</meta>

### WALDENSIANS.

For this reason, the Count <note>Count Taffin, the commander of the Duke’s forces mentioned on the previous page.</note> traveled to **Lucerna** <note>Val Pellice, also known as the Val di Luserna.</note> and to the Court; he summoned the Ministers, **Syndics** <note>original: "Sindicos"; local magistrates or community leaders.</note>, and other prominent figures of the churches of the **Val Lucerna** to meet. He attempted to persuade them that the army had not moved into the **Val Perosa** <note>original: "Dal Peruse"; a valley where Waldensian and Catholic populations lived in close proximity.</note> for reasons of religion, but only to punish certain rebels. He argued that they should therefore not involve themselves in this business, but rather leave the matter to the Duke.

Whereupon the **Evangelicals** <note>The Waldensian Protestants.</note> pointed out to the Count that this was indeed a war concerning religion, and that for this reason, they could in no way abandon their fellow brothers. At the same time, they pleaded with him to work toward a solution so that this dispute might be settled without further bloodshed.

However, after much dispute, those of the Val Perosa finally decided among themselves to demolish the aforementioned churches, on the condition that the great military force—about ten thousand men strong—would then withdraw; and that once the army had departed, they might rebuild the churches again, according to what had been promised to them.

But while they were busy with the work of tearing down the church tower at **Pinasca** <note>original: "Pinasche"; a village in the Val Perosa.</note>, and settling all disputes with **Lord Sillan**—who had ordered the Reformed inhabitants of **San Germano** <note>original: "St. Germain"; San Germano Chisone.</note> to meet him at **Dubbione** <note>original: "Dublon".</note>—news arrived in great haste that the army had been significantly reinforced and had already launched an attack on San Germano.

# Chronicle of the

When the representatives of the **Reformed** <note>The Waldensian Protestants.</note> heard of these developments, they abandoned the **treaties** <note>original: "Tractaten"; refers here to the ongoing diplomatic negotiations.</note> they had begun and hurried home. There, they discovered that their people had fought against the Enemy for some time during the night, striking down many of them, while not losing a single man on their own side.

**Count Taffin** excused this hasty and unforeseen raid as best he could, and he made a new start with those negotiations with the Reformed that had been set aside. The demolition <note>The destruction of the Waldensian churches, a condition imposed by the Duke's forces.</note> was then recognized and accepted based on the previously mentioned condition, though this was done against the better judgment of many.

Once the churches had been demolished, they requested of the Count that now, according to his promise and the concluded agreement, the army should be led away. But he would not agree to this; [he insisted] that the Reformed must first lay down their arms and level all their erected **ramparts** <term>Schanssen: temporary defensive fortifications made of earth and timber</term>.

But because they perceived the goal he sought—namely, that he might bring that small valley, which includes **St. Germain** and **Pramol** <note>San Germano Chisone and Pramollo, two key Waldensian strongholds.</note>, under his power—they decided to defend themselves in their **trenches** <term>Loop-graven: defensive ditches or entrenchments</term> to the last man. Meanwhile, they did not cease to earnestly request the Count that he would do them no further violence, and that he should not advance further upon them or expose himself: for such a situation would be very regrettable for them <note>The Waldensians are warning the Count that if he advances, they will be forced to fire upon him, despite their desire for peace.</note>.

-># WALDENSIANS<-

<margin>P 3</margin>

But all this was little regarded; and some of the Enemy came close to their fortified positions and threw many mocking and abusive words at them: one of the **Reformed** <note>The Waldensian Protestants.</note>, embittered by this, and without the will or approval of the others, shot down a **Captain** <term>Hooftman: a military leader or commander</term> of the Enemy.

Because of this, they were attacked on all sides by the entire force; but they put up such a brave resistance that the Enemies, after they had suffered great damage, were forced to withdraw: but in their retreat, they burned many houses and barns.

The next day, the Reformed were concerned that they might be attacked anew and even more severely; they therefore prepared themselves once again for defense. But as the Enemy Army, making poor progress and facing intolerable hardships because of the great cold and deep snow, had fought for a long time against the good people, they finally withdrew from the area.

Since **Count Taffin** <note>The commander of the Duke of Savoy's forces.</note> had now sufficiently found that such a war was very harmful not only to the Reformed but also especially to the **Papists** <note>A contemporary term for Roman Catholics.</note> themselves, he persuaded the Reformed of the **Perosa Valley** <note>Val Perosa, one of the primary Waldensian valleys in the Piedmont.</note> that they should send some representatives with him to **Turin** to appeal to the **Duke** <note>Charles Emmanuel I, Duke of Savoy.</note> for mercy.

After they had been in Turin for an entire month, they were finally allowed to appear before the Duke; who [told] them (the inhabi-

The **Lucerna Valley** <note>Val Luserna</note> also had little rest in this year of 1624. In the following years, 1625 and 1626, the **Churches** faced no general **persecution**; however, they were attacked in various ways by many different **members of religious orders** <term>Ordens-lieden: monks or friars belonging to specific Catholic orders, such as the Jesuits or Franciscans</term>, especially through **disputes** in which, nonetheless, they always held the upper hand. It was also a source of great sorrow to the **Reformed** to find that many of their **youths** were being secretly stolen by the **Monks** and placed into **cloisters**, from which they could by no means be rescued.

In the year 1627, on the 9th of June, many **Evangelicals** <note>A term used here for the Protestant Waldensians</note> at **Lucerna**, **Bobbio**, **Campiglione**, and **Fenile** <note>Original: Bubiane, Campillon, and Femil</note> were captured and taken to **Cavour** <note>Original: Cabur</note>, and from there to the **Castle of Villafranca**. Because of this, the **Churches** of the **Lucerna Valley** made a complaint; they sent several representatives to the **Nobility** of this Valley, praying that they might be left in possession of their **liberties**, and that the prisoners might be released once again.

But since they could obtain nothing from the **Noblemen**, they appealed to the **Duke** <note>Charles Emmanuel I, Duke of Savoy</note> himself for the release of their kin; yet there too, all their pleading was in vain. For on the 12th of July, they were led away to **Turin** <note>The capital of the Duchy of Savoy</note> in a very mocking manner.

At this time, those remaining in the **Marquisate of Saluzzo** <note>Original: Saluces</note> and in the **Stura Valley** <note>Original: Dal Sture</note> were also seized as prisoners and robbed of their property. This even happened to those who had promised to attend the **Mass** and actually did so. For they were accused of attending the **Mass** too infrequently and occasionally going to [Protestant] **Sermons**.

Shortly thereafter, certain people entered the **Lucerna Valley** and robbed the **Evangelicals** of everything they could, under the pretext that they had forfeited their rights, some in one place and some in another.

When such robbery had continued for a long time, the **Reformed** made their rightful complaint to the **Duke**, who gave them this answer: namely, that he had known nothing of such "novelties" <term>nieuwigheydt: here referring to unprecedented or illegal harassments that violated existing treaties</term> and desired that they should remain unmolested, according to the treaty made and the **liberty** granted to them.

At the beginning of the year 1628, the **Reformed** in the **Lucerna Valley** were urged by certain **Lords** to send some representatives from their number to **Turin**, in order to obtain a complete renewal of all their affairs from the **Duke**.

<meta>This page continues the narrative regarding the efforts of the Catholic Counter-Reformation in the Waldensian valleys. The previous page ended by mentioning that monastic orders took advantage of a period of famine to pressure the Reformed (Waldensian) believers to abandon their faith.</meta>

### Chronicle of the

...and there received this office of **Prior** <term>Priorschap|The office or rank of a prior, the head of a religious house or monastery</term>, because he promised to propagate the **Popish Religion** <note>original: "Paepsche Religie." A contemporary Protestant term for Roman Catholicism, often used with a derogatory or polemical tone.</note> to the utmost of his ability in the **Valleys**, and in opposition to it, to do damage to the **Reformed**, or, if possible, to abolish them entirely.

And so that he might now fulfill these promises, he resolved to build new monasteries in the **Lucerne Valley** <note>Val Lucerna, one of the core valleys of the Waldensian territory in Piedmont.</note> and to ordain monks to serve in them.

He first persuaded his father to sell his house and other associated buildings at **Cour** to serve as a monastery for the **Franciscan Monks**.

These monks were appointed there in this same year, **1628**, and provided with all necessities in such a manner that they could richly distribute provisions to all the **Papists**, who were still only few in number there.

But they did this not so much for the sake of the **Papists**, to feed them in this time of high prices <note>Refers to the severe famine and scarcity caused by war and crop failure mentioned earlier in the text.</note>, as for the sake of the **Reformed**, to lure them by this means and bring them to apostasy.

But since no one presented or offered themselves because of this, they—and especially the **Prior's mother**—visited all the poor in their houses and exhorted them not to refuse the great alms being distributed by the neighboring monks, but to receive them and turn themselves to the **Catholic Religion**.

<meta>catchword: But</meta>

<summary>This page describes the 1628 establishment of a Franciscan monastery in the Lucerne Valley as a tool for the Counter-Reformation. It highlights how the Catholic authorities used food aid during a famine to attempt to convert the impoverished Waldensian (Reformed) population.</summary>

<keywords>Counter-Reformation, Lucerne Valley, Franciscans, famine, proselytism, Waldensians, apostasy, 1628</keywords>
